## 16. Wire the Identity Gate So Something Actually Runs It

[`corpus_resolution_snapshot.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/corpus_resolution_snapshot.py) has been the acceptance test for every resolver change since §10, and **nothing runs it**. It is a manual `write`/`compare` a human remembers to invoke, which means the gate protects only the changes whose author chose to be protected -- the property a gate exists to remove. §12 parked the wiring with a real blocker (below) and recorded the follow-up as "unfiled"; this section is that filing, so the park has an owner that can re-open it.

**The park's two failure modes are the design constraints, not obstacles to route around.** §12 tried both obvious mechanisms across two Codex design rounds and each broke on a specific, reproducible case: a pre-commit hook conditioned on the worktree diff validates the WRONG artifact under partial staging and never fires at all in a clean CI checkout with no base SHA; and occurrence keys (`item_idx`/`ref_i`) are stable only while the cache is held fixed, so a tracked-baseline check either misses real regressions (as a WARN) or fires on ordinary TODO edits (as an ERROR). A mechanism that does not answer both is not an improvement on the manual call.

- [x] Chose the wiring mechanism on the two failure modes named here, and wrote down why each loser loses
      WINNER: a CI job (`identity-gate` in [`.github/workflows/todo-graph.yml`](../../.github/workflows/todo-graph.yml)) driving [`scripts/todo-graph/identity-gate.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/identity-gate.sh). Three Codex design rounds; the losers and the reasons are recorded in the script header so a later session does not re-litigate them.
      - LOSER 1, the pre-commit hook, on THREE counts and not just §12's two: git hooks are OPT-IN per developer (`install-hooks.sh`), so a hook gate protects only the authors who chose to be protected, which is the exact property this section exists to remove; conditioned on the worktree diff it validates the wrong artifact under partial staging; and it lives in `.githooks/`, control plane the unattended runner may not edit at all.
      - LOSER 2, a tracked baseline file, because its keys embed `item_idx`/`ref_i`. Not worked around -- DISSOLVED. There is no baseline file: the cache is built ONCE and BOTH walks read that same byte-identical file, so the key set is identical by construction, only resolver CODE varies, and a TODO edit cannot move a key. `DROPPED` and `ADDED` become structurally impossible, which fixture 22b proves by editing a TODO and still passing.
      - HONEST LIMIT, documented rather than hidden: this repo pushes directly to main, so the gate DETECTS after the fact, it does not PREVENT. What it guarantees is ADJUDICATION -- the range runs from the last SHA the job actually PASSED, not the push's before-SHA, so a cancelled or skipped run is absorbed by the next one instead of lost. Prevention needs a pre-receive hook or a protected merge queue, both operator-reserved.
- [x] Implemented the chosen wiring, fail-CLOSED on its own infrastructure errors
      Exit 1 (regression), 2 (usage) and 3 (infrastructure) all FAIL the job; "the gate could not run" is never read as "the gate passed". The job carries its own concurrency group with `cancel-in-progress: false`, because its verdict is about a RANGE and cancelling it drops the only adjudication that range gets.
      - Automation has no human to act on `compare`'s "(GROUND-TRUTH BY HAND)" lines, so a new `--strict` flag makes `GAINED`/`ADDED` fail too: a resolver defect that binds a previously unresolved ref to the WRONG definition looks exactly like a coverage win. There is deliberately NO in-band override -- an approval token the committer can mint is not approval, and this repo's committer is frequently an unattended agent.
      - `--strict` is a flag on the tool rather than the driver grepping stdout for `GAINED` lines, because a print-format tweak would then silently disable the gate.
      - NON-CANCELLABLE COST A PLATFORM FACT TO GET RIGHT: a job-level concurrency group does NOT exempt a job from its own RUN being cancelled, so the workflow-level `cancel-in-progress: true` made this job cancellable while it claimed otherwise. The cancellable group now sits on `validate` alone and the workflow carries none. Adversarial review caught this; the first implementation shipped the claim without the property.
      - The gate now FAILS CLOSED on any protocol-constant change rather than taking a protocol-only exit. `SNAPSHOT_SCHEMA` sits in the same file as `collect()` and `--strict`, so "schema changed but the resolver did not" could not be established without also excusing verdict-affecting edits -- the same constant-beside-logic problem `ALL_BUCKETS` has, so it gets the same answer instead of a second mechanism that is wrong more subtly. §18 owns the extraction that makes a migration possible.
- [x] Fixtures proving the gate FIRES, each mutation-checked
      Eleven sub-tests (22a-22k) in [`test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh), on a throwaway clone with a pruned corpus so the double walk costs seconds. 22c mutates `resolve_ref` into a PARTIAL loss and asserts rc 1 naming `LOST`; 22d reverts it and asserts green, so 22c cannot pass against a gate that fails always.
      - The first run of these fixtures caught a real defect in the gate AND a green-for-the-wrong-reason defect in themselves: `identity-gate.sh` derives its repo root from its own location, so invoking the live script from inside the clone silently operated on the LIVE repo, fell back to `HEAD~1`, found an unchanged closure and exited 0. Two firing cases "passed" without walking anything. Fixed both -- the fixture runs the clone's own copy, and an explicit `--base` that does not resolve is now rc 3 instead of a silent fallback to a different range.
      - 22b deliberately rides a BENIGN resolver comment alongside the TODO edit. Without it the closure is byte-identical, the gate early-exits, and the case proves nothing about key stability.
      - 22g proves the TOOL's `--strict` branch rejects a pure GAINED; 22h proves the DRIVER actually passes the flag, which 22g cannot see. Mutation-checked by deleting `--strict` from the driver: 22h alone flips to rc 0 and fails, every other case stays green. Round 2 of the adversarial review is what found the gap -- 22c's LOST fails with or without `--strict`, so the integration guard was missing.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: run the identity gate automatically instead of on request"`

**Test checkpoint:** the new gate fires on a deliberately broken resolver and passes on an unchanged tree; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` green with the new fixtures; `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green; a TODO-only edit does not trip it.

-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §14`](#14-identity-gate-over-the-whole-symbol-ref-population) -- must land first: wiring a gate that verdicts only part of the population would automate an incomplete proof.
-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §17`](#17-one-shared-cache-schema-validator-for-both-cache-readers) -- split out of this section (item: "ONE validator module both readers call, with the shape rules stated once"); this section wires the gate, §17 makes both cache readers agree about what a valid cache is.
-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §18`](#18-identity-gate-hardening-producer-differential-and-protocol-constant-extraction) -- owns the four review findings this section accepted but does NOT close (item: "Producer differential across BOTH corpora"); the gate ships without them and states every one of those blind spots in its own header.

> **Notes:** The gate DETECTS, it does not PREVENT -- this repo pushes straight to main, so a bad push lands before the job verdicts it. What is guaranteed is ADJUDICATION: the range starts at the last SHA the job actually passed, so a cancelled or skipped run is absorbed by the next one instead of lost. Prevention needs a pre-receive hook or a protected merge queue, both operator-reserved. Two review rounds were spent on claims that were true in prose and false in code: a job-level concurrency group does not exempt a job from its own run being cancelled, and a regex over `ALL_BUCKETS` cannot see a bucket rename because the RHS is spelled in terms of two other constants. Both are now pinned by fixtures rather than by comments.

## 17. One Shared Cache-Schema Validator for Both Cache Readers

> **Spawned-by:** §16 (split)

§14 gave the two cache readers ONE shared RESOLUTION rule and deliberately stopped there. They still do not share the cache-VALIDATION rule: [`corpus_resolution_snapshot._load_nodes`](../../scripts/todo-graph/corpus_resolution_snapshot.py) validates node/item/ref shape and the three ref scalars against explicit `CacheError`s, while [`check_stub_behind_stamp`](../../scripts/lint/check_stub_behind_stamp.py) validates none of it and leans on a broad `except Exception -> rc 6`. So the two disagree about which caches are valid at all, and about which exit code a malformed one produces.

**Split out of §16 because the failure modes are opposite, not because the work is large.** §16's failure mode is a gate that does not fire (or fires on an ordinary TODO edit); this section's is two readers that fire DIFFERENTLY on the same input. §16 is still what makes it matter -- an automated gate must refuse a bad cache identically on both sides, or the wired verdict and the lint disagree about whether the run happened at all -- so this lands after the wiring, against a real caller rather than a hypothetical one.

- [x] ONE validator module both readers call, with the shape rules stated once
      Shipped as [`scripts/todo-graph/cache_schema.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/cache_schema.py) (`load_and_validate` / `validate_nodes` / `check_freshness`), lifted from `_load_nodes` with every Codex-finding comment preserved. Both readers import it (`import cache_schema as _cs`) and neither keeps a private copy; test 23n asserts the import on both sides so the rule cannot quietly un-share.
      - Scope is STATED in the module header rather than implied, and the line is **"fields the readers CONSUME"**, not "the `stamped_items` subtree": adversarial review found node-level `file_path` is consumed by BOTH walks as an occurrence-key component, so an unvalidated list-valued one is truthy, stringifies into the key, and turns a corrupt cache into a rc 1 VERDICT (or a poisoned baseline) instead of an rc 3 refusal. It is now required-string checked. Frontmatter neither reader consumes stays out of scope -- `build.py` owns it.
      - READINESS checks (existence, parse, byte ceiling, empty-array, staleness, population, concurrent-rewrite) are operational rather than schema, and are labelled as such.
      - Codex design review found the lift alone would ship a partial validator advertising a schema it did not enforce, and that NOTHING in the repo validated against `cache.schema.json` (it was inert documentation). The module now implements the documented subtree, and a live probe confirmed zero violations across 5,883 items / 3,676 refs, so the tightening had no blast radius.
- [x] Reject the FALSEY shapes, which the lint currently skips in silence
      `stamped_items: {}` and `refs: {}` are now `isinstance(..., list)` checks, not truthiness, so they raise `SHAPE` instead of reading as "absent". Fixtures 23b/23c assert both through both readers; test 23l mutation-checks the rule by deleting it and proving the lint falls back to the original silent clean run.
- [x] Each caller keeps its OWN documented infrastructure exit code
      The module raises `CacheSchemaError` carrying a `reason` tag and never calls `sys.exit`. The snapshot maps every reason to its single rc 3; the lint maps them through an EXPLICIT `_CACHE_REASON_RC` table (MISSING 2, LEGACY 3, STALE 4, UNREADABLE/SHAPE/EMPTY 5) with no `.get` default, so a new reason cannot silently inherit a fallback code.
      - Every decode failure now routes identically on both sides: `UnicodeDecodeError` (a `ValueError`, not a `JSONDecodeError`) and `RecursionError` (neither) are both caught in the shared reader, closing the bare-exit-1 escape the lint still carried.
      - The lint's staleness check was fail-OPEN three ways (swallowed `os.walk` traversal errors, returned "fresh" on a `stat` failure, called a cache fresh against an empty corpus) and was skipped entirely when the todo root was absent; `check_freshness` is fail-closed on all four and is now the only copy.
- [x] The read is GENERATION-BOUND and BUDGETED (Codex adversarial, two [high]/[medium])
      Added during implementation. The cache is opened ONCE and every later decision comes from `fstat` on that descriptor, not a fresh `stat` of the pathname.
      - TOCTOU: reading bytes and then separately stat-ing the path let a reader parse the OLD cache while `build.py` finished writing the NEW one, after which the freshness check certified stale in-memory nodes with the new file's mtime. `check_freshness` now takes the bound `cache_mtime`, and a post-validation `fstat`/`stat` comparison (mtime_ns, size, dev/ino) refuses a cache rewritten mid-validation rather than returning a verdict from mixed generations.
      - Input budget: `read_text` + `json.loads` materialised the whole document and its object graph with no ceiling, so a corrupt cache could OOM-kill either reader OUTSIDE the documented exit-code contract. A 64 MiB ceiling is enforced BEFORE allocation (live cache is 3.3 MiB, ~19x headroom), read as `limit+1` so a file growing mid-read is caught rather than silently truncated.
      - Round 2 found the `MemoryError` mapping covered only `json.loads`, while the read, the decode and `validate_nodes` all allocate outside it -- and since both readers catch only `CacheSchemaError`, an OOM anywhere else escaped as a bare rc 1, which the snapshot DOCUMENTS as "a prior verdict changed". `load_and_validate` is now a thin normalizing wrapper over `_load_and_validate`, so an allocation failure at ANY stage reports a reason. Fixture 23k5 fault-injects at the read.
      - Round 2 also found the CORPUS was not generation-bound even though the cache was: each TODO was statted once into a running `max()`, so a file edited after the walk had already visited it was never observed, the cache itself need not change, and both readers walked stale nodes and produced a verdict. Freshness now takes two full corpus scans (`path -> dev/ino/size/mtime_ns`) and refuses on any difference, which also catches a TODO added or removed mid-walk. Measured cost +2.6 ms (24.1 -> 26.7 ms).
      - REJECTED from round 2: per-element structural limits (max nodes/items/refs, string lengths). `build/todo-cache.json` is a derived, gitignored, locally-produced artifact, so an actor able to write it can already run arbitrary code in the build; the reachable requirement is that CORRUPTION yields a documented code rather than an OOM, which the normalization above guarantees. The byte ceiling remains the input bound.
- [x] A zero baseline must not disarm the population gate (Codex adversarial, [medium])
      Added during implementation. The empty-population policy above hands off to the lint's rc 7 gate, but baseline validation accepted `total: 0` -- so with an emptied `stamped_items`, `occurrences` is also 0, the denominator equality passes, the resolved floor `0 >= 0` passes, and `lint.sh` renders `resolved=0/0` as full-coverage INFO. Re-recording the baseline during a producer collapse would have made the collapse permanent and invisible.
      - `total` is now rejected at `<= 0` (rc 8, baseline invalid) rather than `< 0`; a corpus with zero stamped symbol refs IS the regression this file exists to catch. `STUB_LINT_ALLOW_NO_BASELINE=1` remains the sanctioned, reported bypass.
- [x] An EMPTIED stamped population is a regression, not an "old cache" (Codex design review, [high])
      Added during implementation: the drafted design mapped both "no `stamped_items` anywhere" and "the field is present but the population is empty" to the lint's rc 3, which `lint.sh` routes to WARN -- and warnings leave the overall lint exit at 0. A producer regression that deleted every stamped item would have stayed shippable, merely turning a false-clean into a warning.
      - The two conditions are now separated by `CacheInfo(population, key_present)`: absent-everywhere is LEGACY (rc 3 WARN, a genuine "run build"); present-but-empty is left for the lint's OWN rc 7 `POPULATION GREW/SHRANK` ERROR gate, which is the mechanism built for exactly that loss.
      - The two readers take deliberately OPPOSITE actions on that one fact, which is why the shared module reports the population instead of ruling on it: the snapshot refuses (a vacuous baseline makes every later `compare` pass over nothing) while the lint proceeds so its population gate can fire. Sharing the RULE is not sharing the POLICY.
      - Test 23j pins the split and 23k proves the hand-off is real: with a baseline recording a population of 1, the emptied cache reaches rc 7 rather than a warning.
- [x] The generation binding covers the CALLER'S walk, not just the check (review round)
      The two corpus scans inside `check_freshness` are adjacent, so on their own they bound a few milliseconds -- while both readers then spend ~1s resolving symbols, and a TODO edited in THAT window left the in-memory nodes stale with a verdict still returned. `CacheInfo` now carries the corpus fingerprint and `check_corpus_unchanged` re-verifies it once the caller's walk finishes; both readers call it and map the refusal to their own infrastructure code. Fixtures 23k6 (mid-check) and 23k7 (mid-walk).
      - The PRODUCER-side mirror of this window is real and NOT closed here -- `build.py` can read a TODO, the file can change, and the cache it then writes carries a newer mtime that makes the stale node look fresh. No reader-side check can see that. Accepted and filed against §19.
- [x] Descriptor close, baseline floors, and schema drift (review round)
      A raising `fh.close()` sat outside every normalization path, so a close failure escaped as a raw `OSError` -- an undocumented rc 1 for the lint and the snapshot's DOCUMENTED "a prior verdict changed" code. Now normalized, and gated on a FUNCTION-LOCAL flag rather than `sys.exc_info()`: that state is ambient and also reports an outer caller's in-flight exception, which would have silently swallowed the close failure of a perfectly successful load. Fixtures 23k8 and 23k8b.
      - `resolved <= 0` is now rejected alongside `total <= 0`. A zero-RESOLVED floor with a positive total let every symbol land in an unresolved bucket while the population matched and `0 < 0` stayed false -- an exit-0 run in which Check 7 examined no function body at all. Fixture 23k9; the pre-existing 14v fixture moved to `resolved: 1` because it had used the zero floor deliberately.
      - `cache.schema.json` gained `minLength: 1` on `file_path`: the validator required a non-empty string while the schema permitted `""`, so a producer could satisfy the checked-in schema and still be refused by both readers.
- [x] Fixtures: the SAME bad cache through both readers, each mutation-checked
      Tests 23a-23n in [`test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh): one fixture per rejected shape, each asserted against BOTH readers in the same sub-test, plus a passing baseline so a rejection cannot be green for an unrelated reason.
      - The oracle is NOT "the two readers agree" -- two readers sharing one module agree by construction, including when it is wrong. Each schema fixture is independently checked against `cache.schema.json` via `jsonschema`, scoped to the `stamped_items` subschema the module actually claims.
      - `gate_seed` now DERIVES the fixture's file list from `identity-gate.sh`'s own `CLOSURE` array instead of a second hardcoded copy. The hardcoded copy is what broke here: the closure grew `cache_schema.py`, the list did not, and seven gate fixtures failed with import errors that read as gate mis-fires.
      - Mutation coverage is PER-RULE (23l2), not one rule standing in for all: each named rule is neutered in a copy of the module and its fixture must stop producing the SAME reason. Asserting on the reason rather than on "still rejected" is what makes it meaningful -- deleting the empty-array rule leaves `[]` refused as LEGACY (lint rc 3, a WARN) instead of EMPTY (rc 5, an ERROR), so the input is still refused but at the wrong severity. The review found the earlier single-rule mutation overstated the claim.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: one cache-schema validator for both cache readers"`

**Test checkpoint:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` green with the new fixtures; `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green; `bash scripts/lint.sh` rc 0; a deliberately falsey `stamped_items` fails BOTH readers, each with its own documented infrastructure code.

> **Test runner:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` | expected: `[test_build] 245/245 passed`, 0 failed -- shared-validator fixtures are 23a-23n. Also `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` (`1287/1287`). Host-side tooling; no kernel `TEST_CAT_*` surface and no `scripts/debug/kernel/run-*.bat`.

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped `scripts/todo-graph/cache_schema.py`: one validator both cache readers import, enforcing `schema/cache.schema.json` for the `stamped_items` subtree plus separately-labelled operational readiness checks.
> - Sharing the RULE without collapsing the CODES: the module raises a `reason`-tagged error, the snapshot maps every reason to rc 3, the lint maps them through an explicit table preserving its documented 2/3/4/5 contract.
> - Closed four lint-side fail-opens: falsey `stamped_items`/`refs` walking zero refs silently, deep-walk crashes landing on the generic rc 6, `RecursionError` escaping as a bare rc 1, and a staleness check that was fail-open three ways.
> - Both the cache AND the TODO corpus are generation-bound (one descriptor, `fstat`-derived mtime, two-scan corpus comparison) and the read is budgeted at 64 MiB before allocation, so a `build.py` race, a mid-walk TODO edit, or an allocation failure reports a documented reason instead of a verdict from stale data.
> - An emptied stamped population now reaches the lint's rc 7 population gate instead of being downgraded to a WARN; the snapshot still refuses it outright, because the two readers legitimately differ on that one fact.
> - `cache_schema.py` joins the identity-gate `CLOSURE` (it can decide whether the snapshot runs at all), and `gate_seed` now derives its fixture list from that array rather than a second hardcoded copy.
> - Scope boundary: node-level frontmatter validation stays with `build.py`; this module governs only the subtree both readers consume.

-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §14`](#14-identity-gate-over-the-whole-symbol-ref-population) -- shipped the snapshot-side validation this generalizes (item: "Baseline format migration, fail-closed on an old baseline").
-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §16`](#16-wire-the-identity-gate-so-something-actually-runs-it) -- the wiring this hardens; must land first so the shared validator is proven against the real automated caller.

## 18. Identity-Gate Hardening: Producer Differential and Protocol-Constant Extraction

> **Spawned-by:** §16 (review)

§16's Codex reviews raised four findings that were ACCEPTED as real and deliberately NOT closed in that section: none is reachable without changing code §16 does not own, and folding any of them in would have shipped a gate whose scope nobody could state. §16 ships with every one of those blind spots named in its own script header rather than papered over, and this section owns closing them. Each is a hole in what the gate can SEE, or in what it costs to run -- not a defect in what it does with what it sees.

- [x] Producer differential across BOTH corpora, so a cache-producer regression cannot hide
      Shipped [`producer_differential.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/producer_differential.py): runs the base and head `build.py` over the base corpus AND the head corpus, projecting each cache to the stamped-ref population `collect()` iterates (`file_path#section.itemrN` -> `{kind,file,symbol}`). Wired into `identity-gate.sh` BEFORE the resolver differential, and skipped when `build.py` is byte-identical.
      - Both producers run against ONE tree per comparison (same `--root` and `--repo-root`), so the git-derived `created_at`/`last_active_at` are identical by construction; the projection drops them regardless.
      - `kind` is compared, not filtered on: a producer that FLIPS a ref's kind removes it from the resolver walk as completely as deleting it, and a projection that filtered by kind first could not see that.
      - A duplicate projection key is a hard rc 3, mirroring `collect()`'s collision rule -- an overwrite would leave a self-consistent projection quietly smaller than the corpus, passing clean forever.
      - Verified by fixture 22n: a `build.py` that strips every symbol ref at the publish point yields `DROPPED` + rc 1, and the mutation deletes them unconditionally so the head corpus holds no live example either.
- [x] Extract the snapshot protocol constants into their own module
      The values moved to [`snapshot_protocol.json`](../../scripts/todo-graph/snapshot_protocol.json) with [`snapshot_protocol.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/snapshot_protocol.py) as a validating loader; `ref_resolution` and `corpus_resolution_snapshot` re-export the names, so no call site changed. The gate proved the move verdict-neutral on its own terms (474 resolved of 1,626 refs, identical both sides).
      - JSON, not a constants-only Python module, on the Codex design review: the gate's inference is "every EXECUTABLE closure file is byte-identical, therefore nothing verdict-affecting rode along", and a Python module still runs at import -- a schema bump could carry import-time behaviour or monkeypatch `resolve_symbol` while every checked file stayed byte-identical.
      - `identity-gate.sh` now splits `EXEC_CLOSURE` (can run code) from `CLOSURE` (must trigger the gate); the `.json` is in the latter only, and reports itself as `data` vs `legacy` so a tree predating the extraction is refused rather than silently covered by an inference that was never true of it.
      - The LOADER is bound to the data: `proto_of` imports `snapshot_protocol.py` and requires its exported schema and both halves to match the JSON it parsed independently. Reading only the JSON let the two disagree -- filtering a dormant bucket in the loader leaves both snapshots verdict-identical, so the differential passes while runtime consumers stop recognising a bucket the protocol still declares (fixture 22ag).
      - Both §16 refusals are replaced: a data-only bucket migration now RUNS the differential (a rename is a real verdict change and must surface as CHANGED), a data-only schema migration PASSES with a loud log (cross-schema comparison is mechanically impossible), and a protocol change bundled with an executable change still fails closed naming the commit split.
- [x] A differential for the LINT CONSUMER, or a delegation invariant in its place
      Shipped the invariant as [`check_consumer_delegation.py`](../../scripts/lint/check_consumer_delegation.py), wired as `scripts/lint.sh` Check 25 (gated on the files that can break it, so no commit pays a second corpus walk for nothing).
      - The load-bearing half is SEMANTIC, per the Codex design review: monkeypatch `resolve_ref` to a sentinel verdict, drive the real `_walk`, and assert one call per symbol occurrence, every sentinel landing verbatim in coverage, and zero self-reached resolutions. The AST half is one call-count assertion kept only as defence in depth.
      - Mutation-checked with the SUBTLE bypass, not the obvious one: an import-time captured reference plus a dead `_rr.resolve_ref` call, which defeats a count-only rule and is caught by all three semantic assertions.
      - A second AST rule (banning bucket-name string literals) was implemented, measured at 28 hits on CORRECT code, and REMOVED; the reasoning is recorded at its site.
- [x] Bound the gate's runtime before the corpus outgrows the CI job
      `corpus_resolution_snapshot.py` gained a two-file `compare <before> <after>` mode that performs NO walk, so `identity-gate.sh` now runs both `write` walks CONCURRENTLY and diffs two finished snapshots instead of serializing them.
      - `IDENTITY_GATE_BUDGET_SECS` (default 600, against the 20-minute job ceiling) is ONE deadline for the whole gate, not one per phase: producer differential, cache build, both walks and the comparison each get only the time remaining, so four sequential phases cannot total four times the bound. Every phase runs under `setsid timeout --kill-after` and is signalled and reaped by process group, so a child ignoring TERM cannot outlive the worktree it is writing into.
      - The deadline is read from `time.monotonic()`, never `date +%s`. A backward wall-clock step would make the remaining allowance GROW -- and this host records three such steps under WSL2 on 2026-08-06 after a suspend/resync, which is why the build-timing test beside it already refuses negative readings.
      - Baseline validation was factored into one `_read_snapshot` applied identically to BOTH sides, carrying a role label so a message still names which input to fix.
      - Each snapshot now records the bucket vocabulary it was written under, and each side is validated against its OWN. Without that, a rename made the base snapshot fail HEAD's enum check as rc 3 before any diff could run -- the gate would have refused exactly the migration the extraction exists to enable. Additive field, no schema bump: its absence has precisely the pre-existing meaning, and a bump would have made this section's own landing commit a protocol-AND-resolver change that the new rule must refuse.
- [x] Fixtures for all four, each mutation-checked
      `test_build.sh` 251 -> 289 tests, all green. Protocol: 22j (data-only bucket addition passes), 22k (a resolver comment is not a protocol change), 22l (protocol + resolver bundled fails closed), 22m (data-only schema migration passes with the stated proof), 22o (schema + buckets together fails closed), 22p (a pre/post relocation fails even though the flattened tuple is identical), 22q (a schema the loader would reject is infrastructure), 22r (object-valued halves are unreadable, not a digest match), 22s/22t/22u (removals and renames refused), 22v/22w/22z (declaration validation and pre-authorisation), 22x (a completed declaration does not wedge the next commit), 22ad/22ae/22af (retirement refusals, including an unreadable emitter), 22ag (loader/data divergence). Producer: 22n. Runtime: 22aa (monotonic deadline), 22y (usage-code budget refusal). Consumer: seven delegation cases covering the captured-reference bypass, the bucket-keyed fallback, the equal-cardinality class swap, rewritten resolved coordinates, a reported-path rewrite, a post-bucket path rewrite, and a data-only bucket addition, plus 22ab/22ac (fail-closed and the clean-tree trigger).
      - Every guard here is mutation-checked except 22aa and 22ac, which are STRUCTURAL by necessity and say so at their own site: faking the host clock, or a real CI checkout, is not something this suite may do.
      - Fixed a real gap the new fixtures exposed: the clone seed derives its file list from the gate's own array, and splitting it into `EXEC_CLOSURE` + an expansion left it seeding ONE literal path, so every gate fixture silently tested the COMMITTED code. That is the same drift the derivation exists to prevent, in a new shape; it now reads both arrays and filters to `scripts/` entries.
- [x] Declared bucket migrations, validated against the edge rather than trusted
      A vocabulary change that REMOVES a name is ambiguous with a rename, and an unused bucket renamed across the pre/post boundary would otherwise ship green and then apply post-resolution semantics the day a ref lands in it. `snapshot_protocol.json` carries `renamed_buckets` / `retired_buckets`; the gate validates every declaration against the real base..head delta before applying any of it.
      - A declaration is ACTIVE only if it describes THIS edge, and it must be NEWLY INTRODUCED on it: one already present in BASE pre-authorises the change instead of declaring it, and is rejected.
      - A declaration retained after its migration completed is inert HISTORY -- its source is absent from both endpoint vocabularies -- so it is skipped, not rejected. Without that, the metadata a valid migration must leave behind wedged every later commit (fixture 22x pins exactly that sequence).
      - Both properties hold with NO history read: comparing the two endpoints' metadata is sufficient. An earlier round accepted the opposite as an unavoidable residual and parked it; the adversarial round that was asked to check that reasoning refuted it and supplied this rule, so the residual is closed rather than documented.
      - A RENAME is REFUSED outright, not applied: renaming the vocabulary alone leaves the resolver producing the retired name -- invisible while the bucket is dormant, and invalid for every snapshot the day a ref lands in it. The refusal names the two-step alternative (retire, then introduce) instead. AS SHIPPED IN §18 the reason given was that names were hardcoded in the emitters and in `check_stub_behind_stamp.py`'s coverage keys; §20 replaced both, and the refusal now stands on the resolver's declared emitted set being executable code.
      - HISTORICAL, superseded by §20 -- retirement declarations made a removal LEGIBLE but the path was CLOSED, every active retirement refused. The only proof then available was a source-text search for the bucket literal, which an indexed or concatenated emission defeats, so a dormant bucket could be removed while the resolver could still emit it. Documenting that would not have protected the shipping path, so the path stayed shut until §20 supplied a provable contract; it is OPEN now, adjudicated against `ref_resolution.EMITTED_BUCKETS`.
      - The rename validation (source in BASE and gone at HEAD, target at HEAD and not pre-existing, targets uniquely claimed) still runs so a refusal cannot be confused with a malformed declaration.
      - The retirement proof is TEXT-based and its limit is stated rather than implied: a resolver refactored to emit a bucket indirectly (an index into the published tuple, a concatenation) emits it while the search sees nothing. Replacing that evidence with a declared emitted-member set is -> XREF: [`TODO-06 §20`](#20-a-machine-checkable-bucket-emission-contract) (item: "Give the resolver a declared emitted-member set instead of raw bucket strings"); an unreadable emitter now RAISES rather than reading as absence, which was the inverted half of the same guard.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: close the identity gate's producer and protocol blind spots"`

**Test checkpoint:** a `build.py` change that drops a stamped ref FAILS the producer differential even when the head corpus no longer contains an example; a pure constants extraction passes §16's gate unchanged; the whole gate is bounded by ONE deadline rather than one per phase; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.

No kernel test surface -- this section is host-side tooling only; the kernel suite is unaffected and stayed at 28326 passed / 0 failed.

## 19. Cache-Validation Reach: the Remaining Raw Cache Readers

> **Spawned-by:** §17 (review)

Split out of §18 on 2026-08-06: §18 is titled and scoped for the FOUR findings §16's reviews accepted, but §17's own review had appended two more items to it. Those two share neither a file nor a failure mode with the identity gate -- they are about the CACHE (who is allowed to trust it, and whether the producer may certify stale content), not about what the resolver differential can see -- so folding them into §18 would ship a section whose scope nobody could state, which is the exact defect §18's preamble was written to avoid.

Split AGAIN on 2026-08-07, before implementation, on a `SPLIT-RECOMMENDED (4 subsystems)` verdict from `section-manifest.py`. The two halves that landed here are opposite in shape and in failure mode: this one is BREADTH (~6 reader files, one already-designed pattern applied to each, the risk being a reader whose documented exit code is collapsed in the process), while the producer-side generation window is DEPTH (one file, a TOCTOU whose fix has to be designed). The section text below already argued they are separable -- "No reader-side check can detect this ... It has to be closed where the cache is produced" -- so reviewing them under one diff would spend the adversarial round's attention on six mechanical swaps and the subtle one. The producer half is now §21.

Narrowed AGAIN on 2026-08-07 by its own Codex design review, which returned `needs-attention` with "Do not implement section 19 as written" and three `[high]` findings, all verified at `file:line` before being accepted. The residual `SPLIT-RECOMMENDED` verdict had been waived that morning on the structured prediction that this was "5 edits, under 15 lines each, independent of one another". THAT PREDICTION WAS WRONG, and recording so is the whole point of making a waiver an accountable estimate rather than an assertion: the edits are NOT independent (all five need a new caller-profile API in `cache_schema` first), and two carry blast radius the waiver never priced -- `query.py` reaches the MCP and watch transports, and `validate.py` both rebuilds on corruption and reads a SECOND historical cache at `:1024` that the inventory missed entirely. Those two became §22 and §23. What stays here is the foundation plus the three readers that are genuinely self-contained.

The reader inventory was verified at `file:line` rather than taken from the original item text, which OVERCOUNTED at "~10": `runner-doctor.py:113` compares mtimes and never parses the cache, and `mcp_server.py:204` delegates to `query.py` over argv, so neither is a raw reader.

- [x] Give `cache_schema` caller-declared validation profiles instead of one fixed contract
      `validate_nodes:301-307` RAISES `REASON_LEGACY_NO_STAMPED_ITEMS` when no node carries `stamped_items`. That is a readiness policy for the two stamped-items readers, and none of the readers routed here consume that subtree at all -- so routing them unchanged would refuse a schema-valid, perfectly usable cache. The module header at `cache_schema.py:64-71` already states the population question is the CALLER's and exposes `CacheInfo.key_present` to report it; the raise contradicts the module's own stated design.
      - A `require_stamped_items` boolean was considered and REJECTED by the design review as the wrong axis: it would still validate an irrelevant subtree while leaving each new reader's ACTUAL inputs unchecked. The shape is a caller-declared profile -- which subtrees I consume, declared separately from which readiness conditions I require.
      - Both existing readers keep the current profile BY DEFAULT, so §17's rc 2/3/4/5 and rc 3 contracts are preserved byte-for-byte. Changing them was the rejected Candidate B.
- [x] Extend `cache.schema.json` to the subtrees the routed readers actually consume
      Without this the section advertises protection it does not deliver, which the design review raised as its own `[high]`. Verified: `stamps_xrefs` and `inputs_xrefs` are bare `{"type": "array"}` with no item shape, so a malformed nested entry passes today. `sections` IS constrained in the schema, but nothing in the shared validator's Python walk enforces it.
      - `test_build.sh` already asserts the Python walk and the schema agree using `jsonschema` as an INDEPENDENT oracle; extend that agreement to every subtree added here rather than letting the two drift.
- [x] Route the two self-contained readers, each with its own DISTINCT infrastructure code
      `todo-reachability.py:142` (bare `except Exception`, so it silently degrades to a fallback) and `check_consumer_delegation.py:312` (raw load, no bounded read and no generation binding).
      - "Keep its OWN documented exit code" is NOT satisfiable by reusing the codes these tools already have, and assuming it was is the trap: `todo-reachability.py:273` already returns `1` for FINDINGS. Mapping a cache failure onto it makes an infrastructure refusal indistinguishable from a real verdict -- the exact defect §17 exists to prevent. It gets a NEW documented infrastructure code, distinct from its verdict codes.
      - A MISSING cache is deliberately NOT an infrastructure error for `todo-reachability.py`, which has a per-file fallback. Absence is not corruption: a fresh clone has no `build/todo-cache.json` until something builds one. Only a cache that EXISTS and cannot be trusted refuses.
      - `check_consumer_delegation.py` is the opposite case, and the ship review corrected this text where it had claimed both readers alike: it has no fallback -- the cache IS its expectation oracle -- so `_drive_consumer:304` returns `None` and `main` maps that to its documented rc 3 ("run build-and-validate.sh first").
      - Test the DOWNSTREAM behavior, not just the load helper: a routed reader whose caller swallows the failure has not been routed.
      - `scripts/overnight/decision-registry.py:67` was listed here as ordinary work and IS NOT -- it lives under `scripts/overnight/**`, which the unattended run may not edit. The run made the edit on 2026-08-07 before catching this, reverted it, and filed both the routing and a real defect it uncovered (923 indexed ownership decisions carry an EMPTY target, because the reader asks for `target`/`target_file`/`text`/`raw` and the producer emits `target_path`/`target_section`/`item_name`). -> XREF: [`overnight-runner-improvements-v10`](../overnight-runner-improvements/overnight-runner-improvements-v10.md) (item: "`decision-registry.py` builds 923 ownership decisions whose target field is EMPTY...").
- [x] A fixture per routed reader, each mutation-checked
      A reader handed a corrupt/empty/oversized cache must exit with its own documented INFRASTRUCTURE code -- not a verdict code, not a shared one, not a traceback. Mutation for each: restore that reader's raw `json.load` and require its fixture to FAIL, so the fixture is proven to test the routing rather than passing incidentally.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: caller profiles for the shared validator, and two readers routed through it"`

**Test checkpoint:** `cache_schema` exposes caller profiles and both §17 readers keep their exact shipped reason-to-code mappings; the routed readers reject an unusable cache with a documented infrastructure code distinct from their verdict codes; the schema and the Python walk still agree under the `jsonschema` oracle; each fixture fails when its reader is reverted to a raw `json.load`; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.

> **Test runner:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` | expected: `[test_build] 315/315 passed`, 0 failed -- routed-reader fixtures, the per-rule mutation cases, the 16-case schema/Python parity table and the AST assertion that neither routed reader retypes a `SUBTREE_*` literal. Also `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` (`1293/1293`). Host-side tooling; no kernel `TEST_CAT_*` surface and no `scripts/debug/kernel/run-*.bat`.

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped `cache_schema.Profile` -- subtrees consumed declared separately from readiness required -- plus `_validate_sections` and `_validate_stamps_xrefs`. The default profile is §17's contract unchanged.
> - `todo-reachability.py` routed with a new `EXIT_INFRA = 2` (1 was already FINDINGS) and post-walk `check_corpus_unchanged`; `check_consumer_delegation.py` routed keeping its `DelegationError`, freshness off.
> - `lint.sh` Check 24 was failing OPEN -- `|| true` plus a `{}` fallback turned every refusal into zero findings on the every-commit path. It now reports rc 2 as a lint error.
> - Two semantic constraints beyond shape, both at zero violations on the live corpus: `sections[].n` unique per node, and `sections[].status` closed to what `build.py` can emit.
> - Canonical doc: the `cache_schema.py` module header; the routed-reader fixtures and mutation cases in `test_build.sh`.
> - Scope boundary: `query.py` is §22 and `validate.py` is §23; `decision-registry.py` is control plane -- filed, not fixed.
> - Ship review (2026-08-07) closed six more, all of them holes in the "validated-clean means trustworthy" claim rather than in the shapes themselves:
>   - `validate_nodes` now refuses a duplicate node `file_path`. Node identity is a consumer dict key exactly as `sections[].n` is, so a decoy node silently replaced a file's real statuses one level above the rule that already refused the same overwrite per node.
>   - A schema-valid cache could still SUPPRESS findings without being corrupt: a file with IO rows but no node fell back to the weak pre-cache rule, and a node omitting a declared row left that section's status as `""`, which reads as "not done". Both are now `EXIT_INFRA` with reason `COVERAGE`; zero occurrences on the live corpus.
>   - The reader's own `__missing__` sentinel lived inside the cache-keyed dict, so a node named `__missing__` switched the whole audit onto the no-cache fallback WITH a cache loaded. Absence is out-of-band state now, and non-canonical node identities (absolute, `..`) are refused so a second spelling cannot alias a real node.
>   - Section numbers are bounded (`_MAX_SECTION_N`, 65535 against a live max of 62). CPython hashes an int as `value % (2**61-1)`, so unbounded cache-controlled numbers can be chosen to collide and turn the set/dict work quadratic -- measured 0.143s -> 0.588s from 4k to 8k colliding rows, inside a 611 KB input.
>   - `check_stale=False` was disabling the descriptor-rewrite check as well as the corpus policy. They are independent: the rewrite check needs no corpus and now always runs.
>   - `PROFILE_STAMP_XREFS` claimed `decision-registry.py` as its consumer in the present tense. It is not routed and reads different field names; the profile is documented as defined-but-unwired, and the `jsonschema` oracle now covers `sections` and `stamps_xrefs` too (16-case parity table), which is what caught the schema permitting an `n` the Python walk refused.

## 20. A Machine-Checkable Bucket-Emission Contract

> **Spawned-by:** §18 (review)

§18's retirement guard asks whether a bucket name still appears in the emitters, by searching their SOURCE TEXT for the quoted literal. That is a real check -- it caught the dormant-retirement hole and its fixtures pin both directions -- but text absence is not proof of non-emission, and the §18 adversarial round that accepted the guard said so in the same breath. This section replaces the EVIDENCE, not the rule.

- [x] Give the resolver a declared emitted-member set instead of raw bucket strings
      Shipped as a DECLARATION plus a runtime bound, not as inferred evidence. `snapshot_protocol.py` builds a `Bucket` StrEnum from the inert JSON (`build_bucket_enum`), `ref_resolution.py` declares `EMITTED_BUCKETS = frozenset({Bucket.UNPAIRED_REF, ...})` and emits members at all 13 former literal sites, and `scripts/lint/check_bucket_emission.py` pins that declaration's AST shape.
      - The set is DECLARED, not discovered. Collecting every `Bucket.<MEMBER>` access was the first design and Codex design review rejected it: `next(iter(Bucket))` or an alias `B = Bucket; B.PATH_ESCAPE` emits with no literal, subscript, dynamic construction or `Bucket.<MEMBER>` anywhere in the file, so the inferred set would omit a member the resolver can still emit.
      - Two halves, and neither is load-bearing alone. STATIC: the checker requires one module-level `frozenset({Bucket.MEMBER, ...})`, never rebound, and bans bucket-name literals (constant-folding `+` chains and f-strings), tuple subscripts, and `Bucket(...)`/`getattr(Bucket, ...)`. RUNTIME: `_check_emitted` in both `Verdict` and `RefResult` refuses anything that is not a `Bucket` member OF that set.
      - The instance check is not redundant with the membership check. `Bucket` is str-valued, so a bare `"path_escape"` hashes and compares equal to the member; the first cut checked membership only and accepted both a raw literal and a runtime concatenation, leaving the enum typing decorative.
      - Overriding `__new__` alone left three routes open, each of which carried a retired bucket in a probe: `_replace`, `_make`, and the module-level base namedtuple. `_make` is overridden (which covers `_replace`), the base is declared inline so no unchecked constructor is importable, and `tuple.__new__` -- which Python cannot take away -- is caught at the public return boundary instead.
      - `classify_ref` and `resolve_ref` are wrapped by `_bucket_bounded(<type>)`, which REQUIRES the declared outcome type. Reading `getattr(result, "bucket", None)` failed open: a missing attribute is the legal resolved value, so a raw tuple carrying an undeclared bucket passed the boundary untouched.
      - The declaration is coupled to the code: every `Bucket.<MEMBER>` named outside `EMITTED_BUCKETS` must be declared. Without that the declaration was a list rather than a contract -- dropping a member while leaving its four emission sites produced ZERO violations, and 22ae could not catch it because it retires a bucket nothing ever emitted.
      - `Bucket` is a `(str, Enum)` with `__str__`/`__format__` pinned to `str`'s, NOT `enum.StrEnum`: that is 3.11+ against this repo's 3.8 floor (`docs/infrastructure/development-tooling.md:133`), and on a supported host it would fail at IMPORT and take the resolver, the lint consumer and the identity gate out before any could return its exit code.
      - Only CANONICAL enum access is allowed. Matching just `Bucket.MEMBER` left a nested `_protocol.Bucket.X`, an alias, and `next(iter(Bucket))` invisible; the iteration form is the worst, because retiring the first member makes that same expression return the NEXT declared one, so a dormant branch keeps emitting under a different name. Two named exemptions only -- one canonical re-export, and a builtin `isinstance(x, Bucket)` -- both of which were abusable as first written.
      - The checker fails CLOSED when a symbol it polices is renamed: the bans are string copies of resolver names, so renaming `ALL_BUCKETS` or `Verdict` silently disarmed them. The binding inventory is module-level only, since a dead decoy inside an unused function satisfied an unrestricted walk.
      - Bucket names must match `^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$`. "Is lowercase" was not enough for the member/name bijection the gate depends on: `ß`, `ς`, `ﬀ` and `ı` are lowercase but upper-case to `SS`, `Σ`, `FF`, `I` and lower back to something else, leaving a valid protocol unreadable to the retirement checker.
      - Mutation-proved, each with its own message: indexed, concatenated, re-typed-literal, computed-declaration, dropped-declaration-with-live-site, nested-attribute, iteration, alias, qualified-`isinstance`, second-re-export, renamed-watched-symbol, dead-nested-decoy and unparseable-emitter (22ah-22aj). Once a member leaves the set, the alias, iteration, reflection, by-value, raw-string, `_replace` and `_make` routes are all refused, and the boundary rejects a raw tuple, a dict, an attribute-less object and a `bucket`-overriding subclass (22ak).
      - `check_stub_behind_stamp.py` carried a SECOND vocabulary -- a fixed six-name tuple for its accounting sum and fixed lookups in its reporting -- so an emitted bucket ADDITION exited 6 and a retirement raised KeyError. Both now derive from the published tuples; fixture 22al exercises the consumer under an addition and a retirement.
- [x] Retire the text-search guard once the contract replaces it
      `identity-gate.sh` no longer reads the emitters' source text: `_emitter_text` and the `still hardcoded in` refusal are gone, and the gate asks `check_bucket_emission.py --emitted-set` instead. `check_bucket_emission.py` joins `EXEC_CLOSURE`, so a commit cannot weaken the checker and retire a bucket at once.
      - 22ad and 22ae both stayed green through the transition and both changed meaning honestly: 22ad still REFUSES (its assertion moved to the new message), and 22ae now APPROVES -- the clearing path this section exists to open. 22af moved to the emitter that actually carries the proof and refuses as rc 3 INFRASTRUCTURE.
      - The gate reads the set with `--allow-undeclared`, because a mid-retirement tree legitimately declares a member the vocabulary has dropped; resolving members through the vocabulary made exactly that state unreadable and reported an invalid migration as "the gate could not run".
      - Bucket names are now required to be lowercase, which makes the member/name mapping bijective without a lookup -- that is what lets the gate recover the name of a bucket already removed from the vocabulary.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: a declared bucket-emission contract, so retirement is provable"`

      Observed 2026-08-07 while shipping this section: `[FAIL] identity gate: TODO-only edit tripped the gate`, with `corpus_resolution_snapshot` reporting `cache is STALE (a TODO is newer than /tmp/identity-gate.*/todo-cache.json)`. 1 failure in 4 consecutive runs of the same tree; 328/328 on the other three.
      - The fixture commits a TODO edit AFTER the gate has built its temp cache, so whether the cache looks stale depends on sub-second mtime ordering. Nothing about the change under test decides it.

The mechanism is gone at source, which is the evidence; 9 consecutive clean `test_build.sh` runs (337/337, then 340/340) corroborate it against the ~1-in-4 prior failure rate.

## 21. The Producer-Side Generation Window in `build.py`

> **Spawned-by:** §19 (split)

Split out of §19 on 2026-08-07, before implementation, on that section's `SPLIT-RECOMMENDED (4 subsystems)` verdict. §19 is breadth -- one settled pattern applied to ~6 reader files -- and this is depth: one file, one TOCTOU, and a fix that has to be designed rather than copied. Keeping them together would have put six mechanical validator swaps and a subtle write-side race under a single adversarial round, which is how the interesting half gets the leftover attention.

- [x] Closed the PRODUCER-side generation window in `build.py`
      §17 made both readers generation-bound and structurally could not close this: `build.py` read a TODO, that file changed, and `build.py` then wrote a cache whose NEWER mtime made the already-stale node look FRESH -- legitimately, because the cache really was newer, which is why no reader-side check could ever detect it.
      - The fingerprint is taken from the bytes the extraction pass ACTUALLY CONSUMES ([`build.py:1203`](../../scripts/todo-graph/build.py)), not from a scan bracketing it. Boundary scans compare equal across an A -> B -> A restore while the nodes were built from B; hashing the consumed bytes makes the record a statement about what was parsed.
      - `_cs.check_corpus_unchanged` then runs before ANY write ([`build.py:1252`](../../scripts/todo-graph/build.py)) and returns a new exit code **3** with nothing written, so a refusal leaves the previous cache byte-identical instead of truncated.
      - An empty corpus is refused there too: `check_freshness` always refused to certify a cache against one, so a producer that published `[]` emitted an artifact every reader rejects.
- [x] Freshness is content-based end to end, so no clock decides it
      `cache_schema._scan_corpus` returns `{rel_path: sha256}` instead of `{abs_path: (dev, ino, size, mtime_ns)}`, and `check_freshness` compares a RECORDED fingerprint against the corpus rather than ordering two wall-clock stamps.
      - The record is a digest-named sidecar, `build/todo-cache.json.corpus-<sha16>.json`, located by the sha256 of the bytes the reader actually parsed. Immutable naming is what lets it be written BEFORE the cache is atomically replaced, so whichever generation survives a crash has its own binding beside it -- a fixed name cannot commit two files at once.
      - It binds the git history too (`corpus_history_id`, tip + corpus-touching commit count): `created_at`/`last_active_at` come from `git log`, so identical bytes over a moved history is still a stale cache. The count is what catches a shallow clone being deepened, where the tip does not move.
      - A missing binding is STALE, not fresh, and an indeterminate git failure raises instead of comparing equal to another failure. Both are the fail-closed direction §17 established.
      - `file_digest` opens first and checks `S_ISREG` + a 16 MiB ceiling on the descriptor, so a `TODO-*.md` symlinked to a FIFO cannot hang every reader.
- [x] A mutation-checked fixture for the write refusal
      Block 25 of [`tests/test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh) drives `build.py` as a module and mutates the corpus from inside `build_node`, asserting rc 3 AND that the prior cache and its binding are both byte-identical afterwards.
      - Two mutants, not one. Deleting the re-verify makes the mid-build case publish (proving the check exists); a second mutant keeps the re-verify and only moves the fingerprint back to a pre-pass scan, which must let the ABA case through while still refusing the plain edit -- that one isolates WHERE the fingerprint comes from.
      - The history case commits an edit and reverts it, so the bytes are identical and only the history binding can see the move; earlier drafts edited the recorded field, which tests string inequality rather than a rewritten history.
      - Fixture caches need a binding to be readable at all, so `bind_cache` writes one through the production machinery rather than restating the rule.
- [x] De-flaked identity-gate fixture 22b
      The mtime ordering it raced against no longer exists, so the race is removed rather than slept around. 9 consecutive clean runs (337/337, then 340/340 after the adversarial round); §20's parked item is closed with that evidence.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: close the producer-side generation window in build.py"`

**Test checkpoint:** `build.py` refuses to publish a cache whose corpus fingerprint moved under it, leaves the prior cache byte-identical on refusal, and the fixture fails when the re-verify is removed; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green. Verified 2026-08-07: test_build.sh 340/340, test-tooling.sh 1293/1293, `lint.sh` 0 errors, `build-and-validate.sh` 8/8 checks.

> **Test runner:** N/A (host tooling -- no kernel/usermode test surface) | validation: `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` (340/340, block 25 is this section) and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` (1293/1293).

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped: the producer-side generation window closed in [`build.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/build.py) (fingerprint of the CONSUMED bytes, re-verified before any write, new exit code 3, atomic sidecar-then-cache publish) plus the content-based freshness rule in [`cache_schema.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/cache_schema.py) that replaced the mtime ordering.
> - How it integrates: one rule, one implementation -- the producer records exactly what `_scan_corpus` computes and `check_freshness` reads, so producer and reader cannot disagree about whether the corpus moved.
> - Downstream: every cache reader gains clock-independent freshness; `build-and-validate.sh` now propagates `build.py`'s status instead of flattening it to 1; a cache copied without its binding is refused rather than trusted.
> - Canonical doc: [docs/infrastructure/todo-metadata.md](../../docs/infrastructure/todo-metadata.md) "The Corpus Binding".
> - Scope boundary: this bounds whether the PRODUCER may certify a cache. Which readers must validate at all is §19's, and the reader-side generation binding it mirrors is §17's.
> - Known residual: the identity gate runs the BASE worktree's `cache_schema.py`, so the CI run at the landing commit still walks with the old mtime validator -- a single self-healing run, unavoidable for any change to the reader closure, and not repairable by shipping the change twice.

## 22. `query.py` Fail-Closed Through Every Transport

> **Spawned-by:** §19 (split)

Split out of §19 on 2026-08-07 by that section's Codex design review, which rated it `[high]`. §19 had listed `query.py` as one of five readers to route and flagged it as "the one judgment call". The review showed it is not one call but a TRANSPORT problem: routing the reader fail-closed is pointless while the surfaces in front of it convert a nonzero exit back into a clean answer. That is a different failure mode from the rest of §19 and a different blast radius -- MCP agents, the runner, and watch-mode consumers -- so it gets its own review round rather than riding along with three mechanical swaps.

- [x] Route `query.py` through the shared validator, fail-closed
      `_validate_nodes` is DELETED. `query.py` now loads through `cache_schema.load_and_validate(..., profile=PROFILE_QUERY)` and refuses with rc 2 plus a machine-readable `cache-unusable` body (`query.py` `_refuse` / `CacheRefused`, and `_build_ctx` inside `main`).
      - `PROFILE_QUERY` declares FOUR subtrees, because the reader consumes four: `sections`, the new `sections.depends_on` group shape, `stamps_xrefs`, and the new `node_fields` (`cache_schema.PROFILE_QUERY`). Two new validators back the latter two (`_validate_section_deps`, `_validate_node_fields`).
      - The design review rated the original one-subtree plan `[high]`: it would have left `depends_on`, `satisfies`, `inputs_xrefs` and `file_patterns` unchecked while claiming the reader was routed. The concrete cost was `query.py:464`, where `_safe_list(n.get("depends_on"))` turned a scalar into `[]` and reported a TODO with unmet dependencies as READY.
      - It also replaced the load path, not just the node check: the old `load_or_rebuild_cache` ended in a bare `json.loads(read_text())` with no bounded read, generation binding or freshness, so validating its output would have been routing in name only. `check_corpus_unchanged` now runs after the walk, before emitting.
      - `sections[].depends_on` gets its own subtree rather than joining `_validate_sections`, so `PROFILE_SECTIONS` (`todo-reachability.py`, which reads only `n`/`status`) is not held to a contract it does not consume. §19 recorded `validate.py` as that item shape's only consumer; `query.py:357-366` opens the same groups, which this section found and corrected.
      - Three INDEX-COLLISION rules ship alongside the per-node walk, all closing one false-completeness class -- a later entry overwriting an earlier one in an index every edge resolves through, so a reference naming the first silently binds to the second at rc 0. `file_path` uniqueness alone caught none of them: duplicate node `id`, duplicate derived `(domain, TODO-number)` resolver key, and an edge naming a bare filename stem that several files carry.
      - The stem rule refuses the EDGE, not the layout: duplicate stems across domains are supported, so a cache carrying them answers normally until something actually references one ambiguously. `_build_stem_collisions` already computed the ambiguity and its docstring claimed the reader refused it -- but its only consumer was the target a HUMAN types, so the graph could be wrong while the CLI politely refused the same name.
      - `resolver_key` is PUBLIC and `validate.build_path_index` calls it, so the validator and the resolver cannot disagree about what collides. They already had: the index hardcoded domain prefixes `00-` through `18-` while a mirrored copy matched any two digits, which would have refused a domain-19 collision the resolver never creates.
      - The RESOLVER itself stopped guessing: `build_path_index` records `dn_collisions` / `stem_collisions` / `stem_paths` instead of flattening, and `resolve_xref_target` returns None for an ambiguous binding rather than an arbitrary survivor. That downgrades a WRONG binding to an ordinary unresolved-XREF finding the graph validator already reports -- and it surfaced two real ones in the live corpus, where a bare `TODO-A` was binding arbitrarily among three `TODO-A-*` files.
      - Specific reference forms all still resolve, which is the half that keeps the rule honest: a fully-spelled stem names one file, a domain-qualified path names one file, a bare letter token resolves against the SOURCE's domain exactly as bare `TODO-NN` does, and an id always wins.
- [x] Make the MCP transport propagate the failure instead of swallowing it
      `buf.getvalue() or "[]"` is gone. `_call_query` now captures `main()`'s RETURN code as well as `SystemExit.code` -- the return value was previously discarded entirely -- and routes both through `_envelope_or_body` (`mcp_server.py` `_call_query` / `_envelope_or_body`).
      - A refusal surfaces as a structured error; a genuine empty result set still returns `[]`. `_looks_like_error_envelope` is what keeps those two apart, so query.py's own richer `reason`/`detail` passes through rather than being re-wrapped.
      - The refusal body is written to STDOUT by `_refuse`, which is the deeper half of the fix: with stdout captured in-process, a stderr-only refusal is exactly what left the buffer empty for `or "[]"` to convert.
- [x] Decide and pin watch-mode behavior after a failed rebuild
      DECIDED: refuse the tick, keep the watcher alive (`query.py` `_watch_tick` inside `main`). Serving the previous cache publishes a graph that predates the edit which broke the build; killing the watcher makes a half-saved TODO end an interactive session. Recovery needs no restart.
      - `_rebuild_cache` returns a TRI-STATE. An attempted-and-FAILED rebuild refuses; a SKIPPED one (out-of-repo `--cache`, absent `build.py`) attempted nothing and must not. Collapsing those two made every inspection-mode watcher serve nothing at all -- a regression this section introduced and its own new fixture caught.
- [x] Fixtures across every transport, each mutation-checked
      31 new/rewritten cases in `tests/test_build.sh`; `test_build` 367/367, `test-tooling` 1293/1293, `test_query_bounds` 22/22. Three pre-existing fixtures ASSERTED the old silent-drop behavior (`total_nodes == 2` after five rows were dropped) and were flipped to assert refusal.
      - Mutations: reverting `_validate_nodes` narrows 3 rows to 2; restoring `or "[]"` turns a body-less failure back into `[]`; nine new per-rule cases neuter each new validator line individually.
      - The watch rule is pinned by BEHAVIOR (refusal names `cache-rebuild-failed`, no stale rows, recovery without restart) rather than by a guard mutation, because the freshness check independently refuses the same case -- a mutation masked by a second guard would pass for the wrong reason, and saying so is the honest record.
      - Freshness applies to the repo's CANONICAL cache; a `--cache` elsewhere gets shape validation with an explicit stderr notice. One shape rule everywhere, and no pretence of certifying a foreign corpus.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: query.py fails closed, and its transports stop swallowing it"`

**Test checkpoint:** an unusable cache makes `query.py` exit with its documented infrastructure code through the direct CLI, surfaces as a structured error (never `[]`) through the MCP tool call, and does not leave watch mode serving a stale cache; the one-row-malformed case refuses rather than silently narrowing the answer; each fixture fails when its guard is reverted; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.

> **Test runner:** host tooling -- no kernel test surface. `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` (384/384), `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` (1293/1293), `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_query_bounds.sh` (22/22).

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped: `_validate_nodes` deleted; `query.py` routed through `cache_schema.load_and_validate` under a new `PROFILE_QUERY` (4 subtrees), refusing rc 2 with a `cache-unusable` body on stdout.
> - Two new validators (`_validate_section_deps`, `_validate_node_fields`) and matching item shapes in `cache.schema.json`, derived from the live corpus and cross-checked with `jsonschema`.
> - Review added rc 4 (`QueryInputError`): the request, not the cache. Raised not exited (so a watch tick survives), held until after `check_corpus_unchanged`, and MCP `isError` now matches the body.
> - Downstream: the MCP tool call returns a structured error instead of `[]`; watch mode refuses a failed-rebuild tick, stays alive, and coalesces an event burst into one tick over a raw descriptor.
> - Canonical doc: [docs/infrastructure/todo-metadata.md](../../docs/infrastructure/todo-metadata.md) "Reader Profiles and Fail-Closed Behavior" -- profile set, exit-code table, freshness boundary.
> - Scope boundary: `requires_history` stays FALSE by design (§24 owns it); `validate.py` recovery remains §23's; three residuals (watch guard, FastMCP `isError`, AST wiring pin) are documented at their fixtures.

> **Verified:** 2026-08-08 | commit `8783f048d` | 5/5 items | build OK | test_build.sh 384/384, test-tooling.sh 1293/1293, test_query_bounds.sh 22/22, lint 0 errors, build-and-validate 8/8, 28326 kernel + 17 user-mode tests, smoke boot 2.650s
> **Accepted:** [H] domain-qualified references still bind to an arbitrary directory: `01-b/INDEX.md` resolves to `todo/01-a/INDEX.md`, and reversing cache order reverses the answer (RESOLVED 2026-08-13 in §26, commit `0c5928fe6`) -> XREF: 00-infrastructure/TODO-06 §26 (item: "Index domain CODES to their full set of parent directories" at line 1353)

## 23. `validate.py` Rebuild Recovery and the Baseline Cache

> **Spawned-by:** §19 (split)

Split out of §19 on 2026-08-07 by that section's Codex design review, which rated it `[high]`. §19 treated `validate.py` as one more raw reader to route; it is the only consumer that RECOVERS from a bad cache by regenerating it, and the only one that reads a SECOND, historical cache. Both make its routing a semantics question rather than a swap, and the second one was missing from §19's inventory entirely -- which is itself the argument for giving it a section where someone looks at it directly.

- [x] Preserve ONE rebuild, then re-validate the recovered artifact
      `load_or_rebuild_cache` (`validate.py:161-243`) now routes through `cache_schema.load_and_validate(profile=PROFILE_VALIDATE)` and returns `(nodes, CacheInfo)`. On a reason a rebuild can repair it runs `build.py` EXACTLY ONCE and validates again; a second failure exits 2. No loop.
      - The double parse and the missing ceiling closed together: one bounded, generation-bound read replaces the shape probe's `read_text()` + `json.loads()` plus the return statement's repeat of both.
      - `_cache_shape_is_stale` was DELETED rather than kept beside the shared validator: `_validate_section_deps` refuses the same opaque-string dep group with `REASON_SHAPE`, which is rebuildable, so the recovery still fires and every other shape defect now fires too.
      - STALE is deliberately NOT rebuildable. The rebuild writes back to the caller-named `--cache` path, so auto-rebuilding on staleness would destroy a captured or imported cache for being exactly what it is; it refuses at rc 2 with wording that says a rebuild WOULD repair it and why one is not run (Codex design review, `[high]`).
- [x] Give the `--diff` baseline cache its own profile
      `PROFILE_BASELINE` (`cache_schema.py:298-330`) declares `sections.depends_on` + `stamps_xrefs` + `node_fields` + `node_identity`, and `diff_caches` reads it with `check_stale=False`. A baseline that fails is an rc 2 REFUSAL, never a `graph-delta` finding.
      - Narrower than the current-cache profile by two subtrees: the delta walk never reads `sections[].n`/`.status`/`.deliverable` nor `section_headings`.
      - No cross-version compatibility is claimed. There IS no cache-format version (`schema_version` is the per-node TODO frontmatter version), and shape cannot detect a field that kept its type and changed its meaning, so the contract is the narrow honest one: the baseline must come from the CURRENT `build.py`, which is what CI does.
- [x] Hold `CacheInfo` until the post-walk corpus re-verification completes
      `_reverify_corpus` runs `check_corpus_unchanged` after all nine checks and the `--diff` walk. Findings are BUFFERED and printed only once it passes, so a run that reaches no verdict says nothing about the corpus.
      - `load_file_snapshot` now fails CLOSED through the bounded `cache_schema.read_corpus_file`. It used to turn any read error into `snapshot[rel] = ""`, and an empty string is a TODO with no IO rows and no bodies -- so a transient read failure manufactured orphan-IO-row FAILs at rc 1 against an untouched file.
      - `--fix-line-numbers --write` cannot take the global check (it changes the corpus itself), so it is bound per destination instead, in three phases: verify every destination, stage and fsync every replacement, then publish. It previously wrote a buffer derived from the startup snapshot blind, discarding any edit that landed in between.
      - The review round hardened that write path further: `lstat` refuses a symlink destination (the corpus accepts one, and `os.replace` would swap the link for a copy), the write set is deduplicated by `(st_dev, st_ino)`, staging goes through `mkstemp` rather than a predictable `.validate-tmp` an attacker could pre-plant as a symlink, and a publish failure names the exact committed subset instead of claiming an atomicity three phases still cannot deliver.
- [x] Split the cross-node uniqueness rules out of `node_fields`, REPORT what the module refuses, and CONTAIN the paths it hands back
      `SUBTREE_NODE_IDENTITY` now gates `_validate_node_ids_unique` + `_validate_resolver_keys_unique`; `PROFILE_QUERY` and `PROFILE_BASELINE` declare it, `PROFILE_VALIDATE` does not.
      - Not planned -- the existing duplicate-id fixture caught it the moment `validate.py` was routed. `validate.py` REPORTS duplicate ids (that is its 8th check) and refuses ambiguous resolver keys inside `build_path_index`, so the module's refusals deleted its own check and re-announced a corpus problem as a broken tool at rc 2.
      - `section_headings` earned `SUBTREE_SECTION_HEADINGS` the opposite way: `validate.py:644` and `:678` dereference `h["n"]` unguarded, and `cache_schema` had left the subtree unvalidated because no routed reader touched it. Its bounds and uniqueness are now published in `cache.schema.json` too -- enforcing them in Python alone was the schema-accepts / Python-refuses gap this file already closed once for `sections[].n`.
      - Declining the refusal is only half the job: the review found the validator then exited 0 on a duplicate DERIVED `(domain, number)` key, which every query reader refuses outright. A ninth check, `duplicate-resolver-key`, reports it at rc 1, so both tools now agree on the defect and differ only in the response.
      - The review's most valuable finding was a path one, and it is why this item grew: node `file_path` was validated only as "a non-empty string", so `../../outside.md` and `/etc/passwd` both passed -- confirmed by probe against the live cache. Every reader joins that onto a repo root, and this section added a WRITE bound to the same paths. `cache_schema` now requires a canonical, corpus-relative spelling, and `_commit_rewrites` re-establishes containment itself rather than trusting that another module was called first.
      - `build.py` changed too, in the two places the new grammar made load-bearing: `file_path` is serialized with `as_posix()` so the wire spelling cannot depend on the build host, and `--output` is no longer `resolve()`d -- following a final-component symlink there let the ordinary `build-and-validate.sh` run write cache JSON over whatever the link pointed at.
- [x] Fixtures, each mutation-checked
      `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` reports 419/419.
      - Rebuild-clean; rebuilt-still-corrupt (stub producer, attempt COUNT asserted = 1); a cache this tool does not own refused with its bytes intact; a dot-dot spelling of the generated cache recognised as owned; a SYMLINKED cache path refused, both through the validator and through `build.py` end to end; stale-refuses-without-overwriting; malformed baseline refused with no delta; valid baseline still diffs; mid-walk edit refused with empty stdout; unreadable TODO refused; a no-XREF resolver-key collision REPORTED at rc 1 while the query profile refuses it; the twelve escaping and non-canonical `file_path` spellings, with a Unicode look-alike deliberately accepted; the five malformed `section_headings` shapes.
      - Repair-path fixtures, all added by the review rounds: write-race refused with the edit intact; a refused write publishing only the refusal; a two-destination rewrite giving each file its own content; a symlink destination refused; a hard link -- including one this run never sees -- refused; a link created mid-staging caught at publish; a staging `ENOSPC` refusing as infrastructure rather than exiting 1; a mode failure refusing rather than publishing owner-only; colliding frontmatter ids refusing the rewrite; a destination outside the corpus refused by the write path itself.
      - Every guard is control-tested: the guard is reverted and the fixture must fail with the predicted symptom. That is what caught the review's worst regression -- a publish loop that paired destination N with destination N+1's staged text, invisible because every fixture until then had exactly one destination.
      - FOUR probes in this section could not have failed, and each was caught only by its control. The stale fixture appended PROSE, which changes no cached field. A `sed` targeted a title that did not exist. A hand-written regex for the 250-char cap disagreed with `lint.sh` on leading whitespace. The write-escape fixture put its target INSIDE the repo root, so the refusal came from `ENOENT` rather than the guard. The control, not the assertion, is what makes a fixture evidence.
      - Three pre-existing `--diff` fixtures were repaired rather than exempted: they hand-crafted BOTH caches over file paths that existed nowhere, which only worked while the current cache went in unvalidated. The CURRENT side is now built by `build.py` from a real corpus; only the baseline stays hand-crafted, which is what a historical artifact is.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: validate.py recovery semantics and a baseline-cache profile"`

**Test checkpoint:** a corrupt cache is rebuilt exactly once and re-validated; a second failure exits rc 2 rather than tracebacking into the rc 1 findings channel; a malformed `--diff` baseline is refused instead of producing graph deltas; a corpus edit during the check walk is caught post-walk; each fixture fails when its guard is reverted; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.
