## 32. In-File Anchor Links Are Checked by Nothing

> **Spawned-by:** §29 (split)

Found 2026-08-09 by §27's consistency review, which caught a dead `](#22-...)` link that `validate.py` had just passed 9/9 over. The `stale-xref` check resolves cross-FILE XREF targets and never looks at a same-file fragment, so an anchor naming a heading that was later retitled is invisible to every gate. Split out of §29 before implementation: that section is about repair mode's exit contract, and this check touches neither the cache, the resolver, nor repair mode.

- [x] Validate IN-FILE `](#anchor)` links against the file's own headings
      Shipped as check 10 `in-file-anchor` in [`scripts/todo-graph/validate.py`](../../scripts/todo-graph/validate.py) -- `check_in_file_anchor` plus the `_slugify_heading` / `_heading_slugs` / `_scan_markdown` helpers, registered beside the other nine and severity FAIL. Scoped to FRAGMENT-ONLY links; a `](other.md#frag)` target stays with the XREF resolver that owns path resolution.
      - The rule is GitHub's: lowercase, strip punctuation except word chars/space/hyphen, then replace EACH space with one hyphen. Do NOT collapse whitespace runs -- a checker that collapses reports false positives on headings containing `/` or `:` separators (that exact bug produced 6 confident wrong hits before the rule was corrected against the real headings).
      - Strip inline code spans PER LINE before matching, not document-wide: an unbalanced backtick anywhere earlier shifts the pairing, and a whole-document strip then leaves prose examples in place and reports them as dead links (observed while §29 was being written -- two examples in that body were flagged until the strip was scoped to the line).
      - UNDERSCORES SURVIVE the slug, and that is load-bearing. The design review recommended stripping inline markup generally; CommonMark forbids intraword `_` emphasis, so GitHub renders `boot_info` literally and 13 live anchors depend on the underscore being kept. Rejected with that evidence -- stripping would have manufactured 13 false FAILs.
      - Slug collisions allocate github-slugger style (append `-1`, `-2`, then RE-CHECK), so a file carrying `Foo`, `Foo` and a literal `Foo-1` resolves as GitHub does. Headings tolerate 1-3 space indentation and trailing closing-ATX `##`.
- [x] The check runs over the whole corpus before it is declared clean
      Census 2026-08-09 over 279 files / 159 in-file links: **6 dead, 0 false positives**, every hit in `TODO-08-automation-hardening.md` across 2 distinct anchors. Both were triaged to root cause and repaired in this commit: `#3-...-hard-gate--state-file--...` had lost a `/`-to-`:` retitle (5 occurrences), and `#25-observer-step-1313-5-...` was a hand-guessed slug for `Step-13/13.5` (GitHub deletes the `.` rather than hyphenating it).
- [x] Fixture per slug hazard, mutation-checked
      Five sub-tests in [`scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh). 8n is one end-to-end TODO carrying every hazard at once whose oracle asserts EXACTLY ONE finding, so it proves both directions in a single run; 8n2 exercises the helpers directly for the hazards that would otherwise bind the test to `build.py`'s heading parser (closing ATX, indented headings, collisions, multi-line HTML comments, escape parity, fence run-length, code-span delimiter runs); 8n2b pins the resource bounds. Two mutations: collapsing whitespace runs turns the `/` heading into a false positive, and reverting the registry entry leaves the dead anchor unreported.
      - The adversarial round found three real false-NEGATIVE paths, all reproduced before fixing: a fence closed on a shorter run than its opener (so code read as prose and the real closer re-opened a fence over the rest of the file), a one-character escape lookbehind that suppressed the live `]` in an even backslash run, and a code-span regex that paired unequal backtick runs.
      - Resource bounds landed with them: HTML-comment stripping went from a quadratic per-comment rebuild (100k comments in 0.67 MiB measured 1.14s) to a linear cursor scan at 0.027s, and findings now collapse per distinct fragment with a 20-fragment cap that still reports the exact total.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: in-file anchor links validated against the file's own headings"`

**Test checkpoint:** a file with a dead `](#anchor)` is reported by `validate.py`; the corpus passes with zero false positives on headings bearing `/`, `:` and inline code; the fixtures fail when the check is reverted; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` green.

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped: check 10 `in-file-anchor` in `validate.py`, fragment-only scope, FAIL severity; cross-file `path#frag` targets stay with the XREF resolver.
> - Census before declaring clean: 279 files, 159 in-file links, 6 dead, 0 false positives -- all 6 in TODO-08, both anchors repaired here.
> - Rejected from the design review: stripping inline markup from slugs (13 live anchors carry underscores) and excluding 4-space-indented lines as code.
> - Nine Codex rounds fixed false-NEGATIVE paths in the SCANNER, not the rule: fence closers, escape parity, code-span runs, HTML-comment precedence, link titles and rendered heading text each hid a dead anchor or invented a live one.
> - Stated limits, both with 0 corpus instances: a code span spanning LINES is not detected (the whole-document strip that would catch it is the measured failure this rule replaced), and a MISMATCHED `_` emphasis run is left literal where GitHub consumes part of it.
> - Bounded on hostile input: scanning is linear (128 KiB of unclosed `<!--` went 3.21s -> 0.000s) and findings collapse per fragment with an exact weighted total.
> - Downstream (HISTORICAL, closed by §36): `build.py`'s section walk read `## N.` out of fences when this section shipped. The "0 live instances" reading here was taken with a naive fence toggle and did NOT hold -- §36 measured malformed fences in two live files. §36 owns the shipped cache producer; §38 owns the remaining parsers; §39 owns container-awareness.

> **Test runner:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` -- 546/546 pass, of which 5 are this section's (`Sub-test 8n`: the all-hazards fixture asserting exactly one finding; `8n2`: the slug/scan helpers; `8n2b`: linear scanning + bounded findings; `8n3`/`8n4`: the whitespace-collapse and check-revert mutations).
> **Verified:** 2026-08-09 | commit `b4f26f6d0` + review fixes | 4/4 items | build OK | test_build 546/546 (5 this section), test-tooling 1319/1319, 28326 kernel + 17 user-mode, validate 10/10 rc 0, lint 0 errors
> **Quality reviewed:** 2026-08-09 | Codex 9x (design, adversarial x4, consistency, perf, re-adversarial x2) | 3H+16M+1L fixed, 0 open | scope: N/A (host-side Python tooling, no kernel surface); parity N/A (no user-visible OS surface)

-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §3`](#3-validator-stale-xref--dangling-dep--orphan--cycle--bat--status--schema) -- the section that owns `validate.py`'s per-file check surface (item: "Commit: `\"scripts/todo-graph: add validator (stale XREF, dangling dep, orphan, cycle, bat, status, schema)\"`"); it is stamped, so this new check is owned here.

## 33. Stamp-Target Capture Splits a Link Whose Label Contains a Space

> **Spawned-by:** §29 (split)

`build.py:225` (`XREF_CLAUSE_RE`) and `fix_line_numbers` both capture an XREF target with `(\S+)`, so a link whose LABEL contains a space is split at that space and only the fragment reaches the resolver. The live corpus writes that shape: `` [`01-boot-platform/TODO-07 §9`](TODO-07-...md) `` in `todo/01-boot-platform/TODO-22-recovery-partition.md`. Split out of §29 before implementation: this is a PRODUCER-side extraction change whose blast radius is every stamp in the corpus, where §29 is a consumer-side decision about one exit code.

- [x] Capture the complete markdown link as one target, not `\S+`
      Label, destination and any trailing section marker are one token, so a legitimate space-bearing label resolves instead of refusing.
      - The grammar moved to `cache_schema.XREF_TARGET_PATTERN` (a complete link OR `\S+`, link arm first), with `XREF_LINK_PATTERN` / `XREF_LINK_RE` derived from shared `_XREF_LABEL` + `_XREF_DEST` components so there is one spelling rather than one per site.
      - §26 already closed the DANGEROUS half in the resolver: a bracket-prefixed token that is not a complete link now FAILS CLOSED instead of being parsed as a path and answering with the file named in the LABEL. That turned a wrong binding into an ordinary unresolved-XREF finding, which is how the one live instance was found and repaired. So this is a false-refusal fix, not a wrong-answer fix -- size it accordingly.
      - 124 such labels exist corpus-wide; they resolve today only because the OTHER extractors hand over a whole table cell rather than a whitespace-split token.
- [x] Both capture sites change together, or the tool disagrees with itself
      `build.py`'s `XREF_CLAUSE_RE` and `validate.py`'s `fix_line_numbers` carry the same `(\S+)`. Fixing one leaves the repair path splitting targets the builder resolved, which is worse than both being wrong the same way.
      - Both now import the shared pattern. Five CONSUMERS also had to change: `validate.py` x4 (`_orphans`, `_stale_refs`) and `query.py`'s `_normalize_ref_token` each split the target at `#` BEFORE any unwrap, which turns `[label](file.md#anchor)` into an incomplete link that fails closed -- so the edge would have vanished from backlinks/deferred/stats/render while `validate.py` reported it resolved. `cache_schema.unwrap_xref_link` now owns that ordering and `resolve_xref_target` routes through it too.
- [x] Prove the corpus-wide effect before and after, both directions
      Record the resolved/unresolved counts across the whole corpus on either side of the change. A capture widening can newly SWALLOW a following token as easily as it fixes a split one, and only the differential shows which happened.
      - PER-CLAUSE, not just counts: 1010 stamp targets, 1010 resolved, 0 unresolved, and the (file, token, destination) triple is byte-identical before and after. The probe carries a control that must fire -- with the resolver disabled it reads 0/1010, so the identity is evidence rather than an inert measurement.
      - The 131 stamp clauses `XREF_CLAUSE_RE` drops corpus-wide are a DIFFERENT defect, not this one: every drop is `no-section-marker-after-path` (`-> XREF: 04-drivers-hardware (no concrete owner item yet)`), 0 are link-form splits. Not filed here -- §33 owns the capture grammar, and a missing `§N` is an authoring-shape question.
- [x] End-to-end fixture where label and destination name DIFFERENT valid files
      The shape §26's reviewer asked for: through `extract_stamps_xrefs`, asserting resolution follows the DESTINATION and never the label. Mutation: restore `(\S+)` and require the fixture to FAIL.
      - Five sub-tests in `test_build.sh`: the end-to-end fixture; the `\S+` mutation; a negative case (malformed/unbalanced, bare-fragment and anchor-only targets all still fail closed) carrying POSITIVE controls so it cannot pass by refusing everything; the query-side ordering; and a bypass mutation that re-inlines the unwrap in the wrong order and must break anchored links only.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: stamp-target capture takes a whole markdown link"`
- [x] Give both capture sites the same trailing boundary (post-ship adversarial, [high])
      Sharing the pattern was NOT sufficient. `build.py` requires `\s+§\S+` after the target, so on `[a](x.md)[b](y.md)` its link arm cannot stop mid-token and backtracks to `\S+`, handing the whole malformed token to a resolver that refuses it. `fix_line_numbers` had no trailing requirement, so its link arm stopped at the first `)` and bound the FIRST destination -- rc 0, zero incomplete-repair counts, and under `--write` a line number rewritten from the wrong file. `(?=\s|$)` gives both sites the same boundary; sub-test 33f asserts they agree per input.
- [x] Bound the label -- atoms AND pair contents, at 1024 each (post-ship perf, [medium] x2; implementation corrected twice)
      The alternation is prefix-disjoint so it never backtracked exponentially -- measured LINEAR at 0.075s/1M and 0.402s/4M label chars, so the reviewer's exponential framing was wrong. Its MEMORY claim held: ~135 MB RSS per 1M characters, ~2.1 GB at 15 MiB, against a 16 MiB per-file ceiling, and reachable only because a whole-link capture makes a multi-megabyte label ONE token.
      - AN ATOMIC GROUP WAS WRITTEN FIRST AND REJECTED. `(?>...)` measured best (flat RSS, 0.271s vs 0.402s at 4M) and is Python 3.11+, against a declared floor of 3.8 (`scripts/setup.sh --versions`) -- and because the pattern compiles at module import it would have taken build/validate/query/render down together on a supported host rather than degrading. The measurement was sound; the portability check had not been done.
      - THE FIRST BOUND DID NOT BOUND ANYTHING. `{0,4096}` counts ATOMS, and the bracket-pair atom held an unbounded inner class, so 4,096 fat pairs still built a ~16 MiB label -- measured 15,006 characters accepted from three atoms. The comment documented a 4,096-CHARACTER cap that did not exist, which is worse than no cap: it stops anyone looking. The inner class now carries its own `{0,1024}`, so the worst case is 1,024 x 1,026 ~= 1.05 MB, measured at 0.002s with a 16 MiB-shaped label refusing in 0.003s.
      - Live headroom: the longest markdown-link label in the corpus is 99 characters and the longest bracketed run anywhere in `todo/` is 531, so the atom cap has ~10x and the inner cap ~2x. Past either bound the link arm stops matching and the target falls through to `\S+`, which reverts to exactly the pre-section-33 behaviour -- a spaced label captured nothing then either, measured both ways.
      - The bound is on the GRAMMAR, not end-to-end: an over-bound bracket token still falls through to `\S+` and is emitted whole, where §26's fail-closed rule refuses it at the resolver. That is semantically correct and memory-bounded only by the file ceiling; the first version of this bullet claimed a ~1.05 MB end-to-end bound, which was false.
- [x] Pull BOTH Inputs-XREF surface forms onto the shared grammar (post-ship consistency, [medium] then [high])
      `INPUTS_XREF_RE`'s `\[[^\]]+\]` label truncates the balanced-inner-bracket label the shared grammar admits, so `[a [inner] label](x.md)` captured as `[a` and produced no Inputs edge -- the same split-brain one producer over. The unwrap happens at the point of use, so `inputs_xrefs[].target_path` still means a DESTINATION and no second subtree changes meaning. Corpus effect: 945 Inputs rows, byte-identical.
      - The TABLE form needed it too, and the first pass filed it as out-of-scope on the grounds that it was the Depends-On table grammar. THAT WAS WRONG: the branch sits inside `extract_inputs_xrefs` and appends to the same `inputs_xrefs` subtree, so an Inputs ROW written with a spaced or inner-bracket label produced no edge while the identical BULLET resolved. The scope argument sounded right and was never checked against the enclosing function -- which is the whole failure, since it was used to justify not doing the work.
      - Both forms also gained the trailing `(?=[`\s]|$)` boundary. Without it the link arm finishes at the first `)` even when another link follows, so `[a](TODO-01-a.md)[b](TODO-02-b.md)` emitted `TODO-01-a.md` as a clean edge and dropped the second link and the `§N` -- reported independently by the adversarial and perf legs.
- [x] Refuse a multi-target stamp clause at the repair site, spaced or not (post-ship adversarial round 4, [high])
      The trailing boundary closed `[a](x.md)[b](y.md)` and ONE SPACE reopened it -- whitespace is exactly what the boundary accepts, so the link arm matched the first link, bound the item to it, and reported a clean update while the builder rejected the same clause outright.
      - The obvious fix -- require the `§N` the builder requires -- was REJECTED on measurement: 70 live XREF targets on item-bearing stamp lines carry no section marker and repair correctly today, so it would trade one hole for 70 silent regressions. The guard tests ADJACENCY instead, and counts the refusal as an unresolvable target so the run still exits non-zero (the §29 contract). It fires 0 times on the live corpus.
      - The two malformed shapes are caught by two different mechanisms, and the fixture asserts the OUTCOME rather than the mechanism: the spaced form hits this guard, the adjacent form never satisfies the boundary so `\S+` hands the whole token to §26's fail-closed resolver.
      - A MALFORMED TARGET HAD TO BECOME A BARRIER, not a gap. Dropping its position let the NEXT `(item: ...)` fall through to the nearest earlier VALID target, so one good clause plus one malformed clause on the same stamp proposed two rewrites -- the second against the wrong file -- and `--write` published both despite the non-zero exit. Recording a `None` position leaves every item after it unrepaired. Measured 2 updates before, 1 after; the control clause still repairs.
      - The same whitespace hole existed in the Inputs BULLET producer and got the same refusal; the table form was already fail-closed (its match is anchored). The adjacency test itself lives in `cache_schema` -- a first cut put a private copy in `build.py` and justified it on the import direction, which was false since both import `cache_schema`, and was the very duplication this section is about.
- [x] Correct three contract claims this change made load-bearing (post-ship consistency, [medium])
      Two copies of "NO READER IS ROUTED THROUGH THIS YET" (contradicted by `PROFILE_QUERY` declaring the subtree in the same file); a stale note that `decision-registry.py` reads `target`/`target_file`/`text`/`raw` (it reads the emitted fields -- that defect was already fixed, and the REAL remaining fact is that it bypasses shared validation entirely); and my own false claim that `validate.py --diff` skips the format gate.

**Test checkpoint:** a stamp whose link label contains a space resolves to its destination file; the corpus-wide resolved-count differential shows no newly-unresolved target; the fixture fails when `(\S+)` is restored; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.

- **The widening made a LOOSE resolver reachable.** §26's label was a tempered dot, which accepts an UNBALANCED bracket, so `[broken and [dest](TODO-02.md)` matched as a complete link. Harmless while the producers captured `\S+` (such a token never survived the label's space to reach the resolver) and reachable the moment they stopped. The first repair -- ban brackets in the label outright -- was WRONG and §26's own fixture caught it: `[a [inner] label](x.md)` must still resolve. The grammar admits a balanced PAIR, with the pair's `]` guarded against a following `(`, which is what keeps §26's nested-link refusal intact.
- **Five consumers pre-split the target at `#`.** Four in `validate.py` and one in `query.py`'s shared normaliser -- all of them turning `[label](file.md#anchor)` into an incomplete link that fails closed. The failure would have been ASYMMETRIC and therefore quiet: `validate.py` reports the target resolved while backlinks / deferred / stats / render omit the edge.
- **`target_path` changed meaning without changing type,** which is exactly what `CACHE_FORMAT_VERSION` exists for (v2 -> v3, the second time it has been spent). A v2 and a v3 cache are indistinguishable BY SHAPE until the first space-bearing label is written, and `query.py` rebuilds a canonical cache only on validation/freshness failure -- so without the bump a stale artifact keeps serving a graph MISSING that edge. Sub-test 25c is the refusal regression and needed no edit: it writes `CACHE_FORMAT_VERSION - 1` and asserts `LEGACY_FORMAT`.
      - The change is OMISSION vs EMISSION, not truncation. Three places (this bullet, the `cache_schema` v2->v3 comment, the JSON-schema description) first said a v2 producer truncated the label at its first space. It did not: `XREF_CLAUSE_RE` required `\s+§\S+` after the target, so `\S+` could never reach the section marker and the clause produced NO match at all. The measurement proving that was already in hand -- the pre-change regex returns `[]` on the control line -- and the wrong sentence was written anyway, which is the failure worth naming: a plausible mechanism was narrated instead of the one that had just been measured.
      - This bullet first said `validate.py --diff` checks neither freshness nor format identity. THAT WAS FALSE, and the post-ship consistency review caught it: `check_cache_format` runs unconditionally in `_load_and_validate` with `require_binding=not check_stale`, so the `--diff` baseline path is STRICTER than an ordinary read, not laxer. The claim was reasoned rather than checked, and the same false sentence had to be removed from `cache_schema.py` beside `PROFILE_BASELINE`. Recorded rather than quietly corrected because the shipped commit message carries the wrong version and cannot be amended after the push.
- **The one live instance was already repaired** by §26's review, so this shipped with ZERO live occurrences of the shape it fixes. That is the honest size of it: a false-refusal fix plus the fail-closed invariant it would otherwise have broken, not a wrong-binding fix.

-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §29`](#29---fix-line-numbers-reports-success-over-targets-it-could-not-resolve) -- the section this was split out of before implementation (item: "Commit: `\"todo-graph: repair mode fails visibly on targets it cannot resolve\"`").
-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §26`](#26-domain-code-resolution-picks-a-directory-by-cache-order) -- the section that closed the resolver half of this defect (item: "Commit: `\"todo-graph: domain codes resolve by directory, not by cache order\"`"); it is stamped, so the producer half is owned here.
-> XREF: [`TODO-06 §37`](#37-one-clause-parser-decides-how-many-destinations-a-clause-names) -- the consolidation this section's review argued for: one parser answering "how many destinations does this clause name" instead of a pattern four producers match against (item: "One clause parser, returning the destinations it found"); no behaviour is deferred there, every shape found was closed here.

> **Notes:**
> - The section as filed was a two-line regex change. What shipped is that change plus six post-ship review rounds: builder/repair boundary alignment, a real label bound, both Inputs surface forms, multi-target refusal with barrier semantics, and five contract corrections.
> - The pattern of the whole section: widening a capture makes previously-unreachable code REACHABLE, in files the diff does not touch. Section 26's loose resolver, five `#`-before-unwrap pre-splits, and the `\S+` fallback were all latent and all correct-looking until the capture stopped truncating.
> - Three claims written in this section were FALSE and caught by review, all of them mine and all reasoned rather than measured: that `validate.py --diff` skips the format gate (it is unconditional), that a v2 producer TRUNCATED a spaced label (it omitted the clause entirely), and that `{0,4096}` bounded characters (it bounds atoms). In each case the correct measurement was already in hand.
> - A scope exclusion is a claim about the code and needs the same file:line proof as a finding. One was filed here saying a table branch belonged to the Depends-On grammar; it is inside `extract_inputs_xrefs`, and the wrong claim was used to justify not doing the work.
> - Rejected on measurement: an atomic `(?>...)` label (Python 3.11+ against a 3.8 floor, and it compiles at import so it fails the whole package), and requiring the builder's `\s+§\S+` at the repair site (70 live targets on item-bearing stamp lines carry no section marker and repair correctly today).
> - Corpus effect is nil in both directions, per clause rather than by count: 1010/1010 stamp targets and 945 Inputs rows byte-identical, repair dry-run unchanged at 404 ambiguous / 50 unresolvable / 75 missing, and the probe carries a control that fires (0/1010 with the resolver disabled).

## 34. The Performance Budget Times Itself on a Clock That Steps

Observed twice on 2026-08-09 while verifying §29, and reproduced deliberately: `[FAIL] build timing invalid (-822ms) -- clock stepped mid-measurement; re-run`, once inside the full `test-tooling.sh` run and once in 1 of 3 back-to-back direct runs. The sub-test is Test 7 in [`test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh), which brackets the build with `date +%s%N` -- CLOCK_REALTIME, which WSL2 resyncs after a suspend or under load.

The negative-reading refusal at `test_build.sh:7618` is CORRECT and must stay: it was added on 2026-08-06 because a naive `-lt 2000` banked a stepped clock as a pass, and "a budget that cannot fail is not a budget". The defect is the SOURCE, not the guard -- a monotonic clock cannot go backwards, so the refusal would stop firing spuriously while still catching a genuinely over-budget build.

- [x] Time Test 7 from a monotonic source instead of `date +%s%N`
      Shipped as `mono_ns()` + `valid_ns()` in [`test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh) (`python3 -c 'import time; print(time.monotonic_ns())'`, ~25ms of interpreter startup per reading against a 2s budget). Test 7 now brackets the build with `mono_ns`.
      - The `<= 0` refusal is kept verbatim and its comment now says WHY it survives: unreachable in practice rather than removed, so a firing means the measurement really is broken.
      - `mono_ns` never returns non-zero, so a caller under `set -e` reaches its `valid_ns` check instead of dying before the guard reports.
- [x] Convert every OTHER duration assertion the same clock step could bank as a pass
      The audit was widened from the `date +%s%N` spelling to the SEMANTICS (any elapsed compared against an upper bound), which is what found the last three: a clock spelling is not the defect, an unguarded subtraction is (Codex design review, High).
      - [`test_build.sh`](../../scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh): the 20k-wide star-traversal ceiling (`date +%s` delta vs `-le 20`) and the two embedded `time.time()` deltas vs `< 1.0` guarding the quadratic lead-regex regression.
      - [`test-tooling.sh`](../../scripts/test-tooling.sh): the `crsw_synthetic_dispatch` 1s bound and the `four_dispatch_gate: D` 3.5s bound (an awk float over `date +%s.%N`, now integer ms).
      - [`test_bridge.sh`](../../scripts/lsp-mcp/tests/test_bridge.sh): the 9d/9e teardown budgets (`-gt "$ceiling"`), plus the print-only `time.time()` elapsed at the completion smoke test so its log line cannot read negative.
      - DELIBERATELY LEFT ON REALTIME, with the reason recorded in each helper comment: `test-tooling.sh` `NOW_NS` (read back against Python `time.time_ns()` in [`build_offload_reminder.py`](../../.claude/hooks/build_offload_reminder.py)), the `os.utime` mtime fixtures, and the unique-ID mints in `test_bridge.sh` / `test.sh`. A timestamp is not a duration.
      - Two boundaries moved by sub-second amounts and both moved toward accuracy: `-le 20` on truncated seconds became `-le 20000` ms (removing up to 1s of truncation slack on a ceiling the measured run clears at ~800ms), and the awk `%.2f` rounding that failed a true 3.496s against `< 3.5` became an exact `-lt 3500`.
- [x] Make the readings safe to feed to bash arithmetic
      Validate-then-subtract is ONE helper, `elapsed_ms <start> <end>`, which validates both readings and prints the delta, or prints nothing and returns 1. Callers write `if ! MS=$(elapsed_ms "$a" "$b")`, so the refusal stays inside an `if` condition where `set -e` cannot fire and no unvalidated reading can reach a comparison.
      - `valid_ns` refuses four shapes, not one: an EXPRESSION (`2000+1` evaluates to a plausible positive 2001 under bash's re-evaluation of variable contents and sails through a non-positive refusal), a bare IDENTIFIER (aborts the suite under `set -u`), a NON-CANONICAL LEADING ZERO (`08` is invalid octal to bash and kills the caller mid-function -- under `test_bridge.sh`'s `set -e` that is before `lsp_9b_cleanup`, leaking driver children), and anything past 18 digits (an INT64 wrap can turn a corrupt huge interval into a small non-negative one that PASSES a ceiling). The last two came from Codex adversarial review, Medium.
      - Sub-tests 7a-7e pin it, and each proves a strictly different thing. 7c mutation-checks the helper. 7d is the WIRING pin -- a sound helper is not the same as a used one -- asserting across all three harnesses that no variable assigned from `$(mono_ns)` ever appears inside an arithmetic expansion; reverting one call site to a raw `$(( end - start ))` was verified to flip it (Codex adversarial review, Medium).
      - 7e is the DRIFT pin, because the helpers are duplicated per script by design and 7d only proves each copy is CALLED: it extracts all three copies, runs the malformed-input vector against each, and requires an identical signature. The signature records stderr presence as well as return status, since the probe runs without `set -e` and a copy that wrongly accepts `08` would otherwise read as a clean refusal; a deliberately drifted copy is proven to change it (Codex consistency audit, Medium).
- [x] Retire the live-gotcha entry once the source is monotonic
      The 2026-08-07 and 2026-08-09 "re-run before diagnosing" entries in `.claude/state/live-gotchas.md` are operator-owned, and an unattended run cannot ask for their removal, so they were left in place and a 2026-08-10 entry was APPENDED that supersedes them: the old message no longer exists, and the two messages that replace it can only mean a genuinely broken measurement, so the advice is now diagnose rather than re-run.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: time the build budget on a monotonic clock"`

**Test checkpoint:** Test 7 reports a positive elapsed time across 10 consecutive runs of `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh`; an artificially over-budget build still fails the 2s assertion; `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.

> **Test runner:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` -- expect `560/560 passed, 0 failed` (sub-tests 7a-7e cover the clock source, the malformed-reading guard, the mutation check that proves the guard is load-bearing, the wiring pin, and the cross-copy drift pin over all three harnesses); aggregate via `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` at `1319/1319`, which also runs the converted `test_bridge.sh` budgets at `118/118`. No kernel test surface: this is host tooling, so there is no `TEST_CAT_*` category or bat.

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped `mono_ns`/`valid_ns`/`elapsed_ms` into all three bash test harnesses; six duration assertions now read `time.monotonic_ns()`, so a WSL2 clock step cannot bank a negative elapsed as a pass.
> - The audit criterion is SEMANTIC, not textual: any elapsed compared against a bound, whatever clock produced it -- which caught the `date +%s`, `date +%s.%N` and `time.time()` sites the `date +%s%N` grep missed.
> - Validate-then-subtract is ONE helper, so no call site can forget it (7d pins that) and no copy can quietly drift (7e pins that); digits-only was not enough, because `08` aborts bash and a >INT64 reading wraps.
> - Timestamps stay on realtime by design (`NOW_NS` vs `time.time_ns()`, `os.utime` fixtures, unique-ID mints); each is annotated in place so a later sweep does not "fix" it.
> - Scope boundary: test harnesses only. Production timing in `build.py`, `query.py`, `identity-gate.sh` and the lsp-mcp client was already monotonic and is untouched.

## 35. A Same-File Stamp Matches Its Own Text, So Repair Answers With the Stamp's Line

Found 2026-08-09 during §29's review, and REPRODUCED with a control before filing. `fix_line_numbers` locates an item by scanning the target file for the literal item name on ANY line (`scripts/todo-graph/validate.py`, the `for ix, target_ln in enumerate(target_text.splitlines(), ...)` scan). When the stamp and its target are the SAME file -- which every in-file `-> XREF: TODO-06 §N` is -- the stamp line contains the item name by construction, so the scan always matches the stamp itself.

Two consequences, both measured on a three-line fixture:

- A LIVE item yields two hits (the item at line 20, the stamp at line 23) and is reported `ambiguous item_name ... refusing to rewrite`. Every same-file stamp XREF is therefore permanently unrepairable, and is counted as ambiguity rather than as the structural defect it is. The live corpus reports 403 ambiguities and the sampled ones are overwhelmingly same-file (`stamp at TODO-25...` naming `TODO-25...`), so this mechanism plausibly accounts for most of that number -- UNMEASURED as a proportion, and worth measuring first.
- A DELETED item is worse: the only remaining match is the stamp's own line, so repair exits **rc 0** and rewrites the reference to point at itself (`(item: "Wire the resolver" at line 22)` on line 22). Observed live in this file: §26's `Accepted:` stamp named an item §29's split had moved away, and the auto-rewrite hook silently repointed it from line 1440 to 1365 -- the stamp's own line.

That second shape is the defect §29 was written to close, reached by a path §29 does not cover: §29 added `missing_items` for a target that resolves without the item, and a same-file stamp is never "without the item" because it quotes it. So the new detection is defeated exactly where the reference is most common, and the outcome is a WRONG REWRITE reported as success rather than a silent skip.

- [x] Match the item name only on CHECKLIST-ITEM lines, not on any line of the target
      `cache_schema.checklist_item_leads()` indexes the item lines of a target and `fix_line_numbers` searches only those. The status class is `[ xX/~]`: `[~]` is live in `01-boot-platform` (TODO-08, TODO-14) meaning "N/A" and dropping it would turn 60 resolvable references into missing ones. Uppercase was briefly narrowed away for consistency with `build.py:865` and RESTORED: that function EXTRACTS shipped items to index code refs, while this one RECOGNISES a line a reference may name, so strictness there buys a tight cache and strictness here would only buy a false "missing" on a valid task list.
      - MEASURED before/after on the live corpus: ambiguous **404 -> 6**, and **334** references repaired that had never been repairable once. 430 of the 706 item clauses are same-file, so the diagnosis holds -- the drop is the evidence.
      - `missing` rises 75 -> 159 and that rise is the §29 contract working, not a regression: 94 of it is reference-quoting matches, and 13 clauses name headings or IO-table rows, which CLAUDE.md's XREF rule already rejects. They are now REPORTED instead of silently mis-resolved.
      - Did NOT special-case "skip the stamp's own line", per the warning below -- and the review proved the warning understated. Restricting to item lines is ALSO not sufficient on its own: a checklist item can carry its own `-> XREF:` quoting another item's name, so the match must be cut at the first reference marker (`-> XREF:` in both ASCII and U+2192 spellings, and `(item: "`). Live proof: `todo/02-kernel-core/TODO-03-kernel-libraries.md:362` bound to the `- [/]` line 349 that merely QUOTES its item while the item it names sits at 313 under changed wording.
- [x] A deleted item in a same-file reference must report, not rewrite
      Sub-test 35a `deleted_same_file` asserts 0 updates, 1 missing, and NO proposed destination -- the stored line is left untouched rather than rewritten to the stamp's own line.
- [x] Fixtures for both shapes, mutation-checked
      Sub-tests 35a/35b in `scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh`. 35a covers FIFTEEN cases: the two required shapes; the quoting-item shape alone and alongside a real item, so the rule is shown to DISCRIMINATE rather than suppress; four fence shapes (plain, a bare four-backtick run, an over-indented marker that is really an indented code block, and a block comment); an item hiding the name in an inline comment; a reference written with two spaces after the arrow; a zero-separator non-item; and five controls that must still RESOLVE -- cross-file, `[~]`, `[X]`, and an item documenting literal `-> XREF:` syntax inside backticks. 35b restores the any-line scan and requires the two ORIGINAL misfires separately -- ambiguity on the live item, and a rc-0 self-rewrite on the deleted one -- so a future refactor cannot satisfy it by breaking repair generally.
      - EVERY fence, comment and lead-stop fixture was checked against the pre-fix code and shown to FAIL there. Two early ones did not discriminate (a nested-fence case, and a clause-shaped arrow case whose `(item: "` stop fired first) and were reshaped; a third asserted the stored clause rather than the resolved destination and "failed" a correct fix. A fixture that passes either way is not a test.
      - The first cut of 35a FAILED against a correct fix because the probe read the stored clause back out of the snapshot, which a dry run never mutates. It now asserts the DESTINATION the repair reports.
- [x] Sweep the corpus for stamps this already corrupted
      ENUMERATED before the fix changed the evidence, which is what the sweep was for: **52** references whose stored line equalled the stamp's own line, across 17 files. REPAIRED the 4 whose intended item is unambiguous (closest-surviving-item ratio >= 0.76, each verified at file:line): TODO-04 2215 -> 2602 and 2441 -> 2495, TODO-07 705 -> 764, TODO-12 1112 -> 421. Each named an item that had been reworded, not deleted, and `--fix-line-numbers` adjudicated the re-points itself -- 4 updates, no new ambiguity.
      - The other 48 are REPORTED, NOT REPAIRED, and that is the finished state rather than a deferral. 40 have no surviving item above a 0.5 similarity ratio, so there is no target to re-point them AT; a bulk rewrite across 17 files would be invention, and the mass rewrite this repo forbids.
      - NOT filed as a park or a new section, on the user-impact test this TODO already applies to itself in §37: a user of Impossible OS hits nothing. It is maintainer-facing bookkeeping, and the information is not lost -- `validate.py --fix-line-numbers` now NAMES every one of them as a missing item on every run (155 today), so the detector is mechanical and permanent where a park would depend on somebody remembering to sweep.
      - The count IS the acceptance test for anyone who later wants them repaired: repairing one is visible as 155 -> 154.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: an item reference resolves to an item line, not to the stamp naming it"`

**Test checkpoint:** a same-file stamp naming a live item rewrites to that item's line and exits 0; the same stamp with the item deleted exits 1 reporting a missing item and leaves the stored line untouched; both fixtures fail when the any-line scan is restored; the live ambiguity count is recorded before and after; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` green.

> **Test runner:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` -- expect `562/562 passed, 0 failed` (35a covers 15 reference-resolution shapes, 35b mutates the any-line scan back) | aggregate via `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` (1319/1319).

> **Notes:**
> - Shipped `cache_schema.checklist_item_leads()` plus shared `FENCE_RE`/`fence_step()`; `fix_line_numbers` resolves against that index and `_scan_markdown` now shares the same state machine.
> - Corpus effect: ambiguity 404 -> 6, 334 references repaired in a separate reviewed bookkeeping commit, missing 75 -> 155 as mis-resolutions became reports.
> - Repair is 48.5% faster than the old scan (97.2ms vs 188.5ms) with an 89-build / 617-hit per-target memo.
> - Container-relative fence indentation is knowingly NOT handled, and is owned by §39 (split out of §36 at authoring time). HISTORICAL CORRECTION: the "0 live instances" recorded here was an ITEM-INDEX census and did not hold for the broader premise -- §36 later measured two corpus files against a real CommonMark parser where a container-exited fence swallowed real headings, and repaired both.
> - Scope boundary: the repair path only. Every other fence-blind parser was filed as §36, which split at authoring time -- the cache producer shipped as §36, the mutating and gate parsers are §38, and container-awareness is §39.

That filing was §36, which split at authoring time into §36 (cache producer), §38 (parsers) and §39 (container-awareness); §38 split again on 2026-08-11 into §38 (gate parsers) and §41 (mutating repair tools).

## 36. The Cache Producer Adopts the Shared Fence Tracker

> **Spawned-by:** §32 (review)
> **User impact:** An author documenting TODO STRUCTURE inside a fenced example gets an `orphan-io-row` FAIL naming a `## N.` section that does not exist, and the only way past it is deleting a legitimate code example. That is not hypothetical: it is precisely how §32's test fixture hit it, and this repo writes TODOs about TODOs constantly.

Found 2026-08-09 by §32's own REVERT mutation, which is the only reason it surfaced: §32's fixture put a `## 99.` heading inside a ``` fence to prove the anchor check ignores it, and `orphan-io-row` fired on that heading instead -- so `build.py` had extracted a fenced heading into the node's `section_headings`. (The original filing said `sections`; that field is the Implementation Order rows, not the headings -- `build.py:build_node` assigns the two from different walks.) `validate.py`'s `_scan_markdown` already tracks fences correctly, so the two halves of the tool disagree about what a heading is.

**The "0 live instances" census was WRONG, and it took TWO successively stricter oracles to find that out (measured 2026-08-10).** The original count came from a naive toggle -- flip state on any line whose stripped form starts with three backticks -- which pairs delimiters by parity and so cannot see a malformed nest. The shared physical-line tracker then found **3 fenced `## N.` headings plus a file that never closes its last fence** in `todo/15-installer-release/TODO-05-github-release-community.md`, where a 3-backtick ` ```bash ` block sits inside a 3-backtick ` ```markdown ` block, closing the outer block early and inverting every later delimiter.

**That repair was itself incomplete, and only a real CommonMark parse showed it.** The tracker applies the 0-3-space rule to the PHYSICAL line, but CommonMark applies it after stripping the list-container prefix -- so a fence opened inside a `- [ ]` item is ENDED by the next unindented block, and the apparent closer becomes a new root-level opener. Under `markdown-it-py` in commonmark mode, **two** files were affected (`TODO-04-release-qa.md` and `TODO-05-github-release-community.md`), swallowing four `## N.` headings and two closing-matter headings between them. Both are repaired here by indenting the container-exiting table so the block stays inside its list item; a CommonMark scan of all corpus files now reports zero swallowed structure.

**The lesson is about the ORACLE, not the fence rule.** "Reuse the tool's own machinery rather than reimplementing its rule" is right for consistency and wrong as a correctness check when the tool's rule is the thing under test -- the probe inherits exactly the blindness being measured. The parser was used as a measurement oracle only; wiring a CommonMark dependency into the tooling is an operator decision and §39 owns the question.

- [x] Publish `fence_mask()` beside `fence_step()` in `cache_schema.py`
      One mask per document, `list[bool]`, True when a line sits inside a fence, IS a fence delimiter, or sits inside an HTML block comment -- everything that is not ordinary Markdown structure. Delimiters are masked deliberately: no adopting caller takes one as semantic input, and masking them makes the mask a correct verbatim-region marker for the mutating tools §38 adopts.
      - Final arities, for §38's adopters: `cache_schema.fence_scan(lines) -> (mask, unclosed_fence, unclosed_comment)`, `fence_mask(lines) -> mask` (a wrapper), and `build.py:scan_body(body) -> (lines, mask, unclosed_fence, unclosed_comment)`.
      - Fail-closed on an unterminated fence -- everything to EOF is masked, matching `checklist_item_leads`, so a malformed file yields MISSING structure rather than confident wrong structure.
      - Reuse the tracker rather than writing a second one. Two implementations of "am I inside a fence" is how the producer and validator drifted apart in the first place.
- [x] Route every `build.py` body parser through the mask
      Five parsers, not the two originally filed; the other three were found by this section's design review and each emits a graph edge, so a fenced example corrupts dependency and scheduling decisions, not just a count.
      - `build.py:extract_section_headings` and `build.py:extract_stamped_items` -- the two originally filed, feeding `section_headings` and `stamped_items`. Referenced by FUNCTION rather than line: this section moves those definitions, so a line number written while editing them is stale before it is committed.
      - `build.py:extract_implementation_order`, `build.py:extract_inputs_xrefs` and `build.py:extract_stamps_xrefs` -- found by this section's design review. A fenced example emits a fake IO row, a fake input edge and a fake deferred-stamp edge; the first two DISPLACE the real table rather than adding to it, because both walks stop at the next `## ` heading.
- [x] Repair the one corpus file whose fences are genuinely malformed
      `todo/15-installer-release/TODO-05-github-release-community.md` nests a 3-backtick fence inside a 3-backtick fence; the CommonMark-correct repair is a 4-backtick outer fence. This is a hard PREREQUISITE, not a side quest: land it before the producer change or the cache loses three real sections.
- [x] REFUSE a document that ends inside a fence, rather than publishing the erasure
      Filed and fixed from this section's adversarial round: masking to EOF is fail-closed about emitting WRONG structure but fail-SILENT at the graph level. Measured 2026-08-10 -- a fixture with a real Implementation Order, a real `## 1.` heading and a real `- [x]` item behind an unclosed opener built at **rc 0** with empty `section_headings`, empty `sections` and zero stamped items, and nothing downstream reported it. An erased node is indistinguishable from an empty TODO.
      - `build.py:scan_body` returns the terminal fence state and `build_node` appends an `unclosed-fence` error, which is non-warning and therefore refuses the cache write. The producer names the malformed file instead of publishing an empty one.
      - The scan is COMMENT-AWARE, in `validate.py:_scan_markdown`'s order: fence state is tested first, so a `<!--` inside a fence stays literal, and only outside a fence can a line open a block comment. Without it a ``` written at the start of a line inside a legal `<!-- ... -->` block opened a fence that never closed -- which, once the refusal existed, turned a comment into a hard build failure. `HTML_BLOCK_COMMENT_RE` moved into `cache_schema` and `validate.py` aliases it, so there is one copy.
      - An unterminated COMMENT gets its OWN refusal, `unclosed-comment`. It masks to EOF exactly as a fence does, so the first comment-aware scan reported only fence state and published an erased node with no error at all -- the same silent erasure, one construct over ([high], round 8). Separate category, separate message: naming a fence when the unclosed thing is a comment sends the author to the wrong line.
      - SCOPED TO ROOT-LEVEL FENCES, and deliberately not further. A fence indented under a list container is not recognised by the tracker at all, so an unterminated one is neither masked nor refused -- reproduced 2026-08-10 (Codex adversarial round 2), where a five-space fence under `100. docs` left `unclosed=False` and its `- [x]` reached `stamped_items`. That is §39's container-awareness, not a hole in this refusal: fixing it here would re-merge the split this section was decomposed by.
      - This is exactly how the one live corpus file was found -- by the fence rule, not by any gate -- so the gate now exists.
- [x] Compute the fence mask ONCE per node, not once per walk
      Filed and fixed across this section's two perf rounds. Five walks each running `splitlines()` + `fence_mask()` cost more than the whole change was worth; round 2 then found the fix still walked every document TWICE, because `fence_mask` already calls `fence_step` per line and `scan_body` repeated the traversal only to recover the terminal state.
      - Measured on the 232-file builder corpus (83,757 body lines): node assembly **290.2ms with five masks against 175.6ms as shipped**, a 39.5% reduction, and faster than the fence-blind baseline -- the original code ran `splitlines()` five times too and now runs it once.
      - `cache_schema.fence_scan` returns `(mask, unclosed_fence, unclosed_comment)` in ONE traversal and `fence_mask` is a thin wrapper returning just the mask. `build.py:scan_body` returns a plain `(lines, mask, unclosed_fence, unclosed_comment)` tuple; each public `extract_*(body)` keeps its original ONE-argument signature and rescans, while `build_node` scans once and calls the private `_walk_*` functions with its own locals.
      - THE CONTEXT IS NOT ON THE PUBLIC SURFACE, and that took four rounds to arrive at. Threading a bare `(lines, mask)` tuple into the walks fixed the cost and opened a worse hole: `zip()` silently truncated on a short mask, and a same-length stale mask misclassified every fenced line. Each hardening round then closed one forgery route and revealed the next -- validated tuple, opaque class, exact-type check, frozen write-once slots, `__new__` with empty slots -- which is the signature of defending the wrong boundary. Removing the parameter ends the class: nothing can be forged because nothing can be passed.
- [x] Prove the cache is semantically unchanged across the change
      Compare every node field EXCEPT the git-derived `created_at` / `last_active_at`, which the repair commit necessarily moves. A differential that shifts any semantic field is the signal that the fence rule caught something this census missed too.
- [x] Fixtures with fenced structure, mutation-checked
      A TODO whose fenced block contains `## 99.`, a `- [x]` example, an Implementation Order row, an Inputs XREF and a Deferred stamp must emit NONE of them. Mutation: restore each fence-blind walk and require the fixture to FAIL.
      - Cover tilde fences, a 4-backtick fence containing a shorter run, and an unterminated fence, so the mask is pinned on the shapes the naive toggle got wrong.
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: the cache producer adopts the shared fence tracker"`

**Test checkpoint:** a TODO carrying `## 99.`, a fenced `- [x]`, a fenced IO row, a fenced Inputs XREF and a fenced Deferred stamp inside fences contributes none of them to its cache node; the fence-blind mutation reads the fake IO table INSTEAD of the real one (displacement, not addition); tilde, nested-4-backtick and unterminated shapes mask correctly; a document ending inside a fence REFUSES the build with `unclosed-fence` and writes no cache; a document ending inside an HTML comment REFUSES with `unclosed-comment` and NOT `unclosed-fence`; a ``` at line start inside a comment neither opens a fence nor refuses, while the bare-marker and comment-inside-fence controls both behave as before; the threaded context returns identical results to per-walk masks on every corpus file; the live cache is semantically identical to the fence-blind producer over the same corpus across all 232 nodes; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` green.

Threading `(lines, mask)` fixed the cost and opened a silent-truncation hole, and four hardening rounds each closed one forgery route and revealed the next. `extract_*(body)` rescans; `build_node` calls the private walks with its own locals.

## 37. One Clause Parser Decides How Many Destinations a Clause Names

> **Spawned-by:** §33 (review)
> **User impact:** nothing today, and saying so plainly is the honest answer. §33 closed every multi-destination shape its review found, and the rule refuses 0 producer-visible clauses in the live corpus. What a user hits is LATER: the fifth producer, or the sixth shape, silently binding the first of two destinations again -- because the knowledge that a clause names one destination lives in a regex three modules use rather than in a parser they call.

§33 widened the stamp-target capture and then spent six review rounds closing one variant at a time of a single question: **how does a producer know a clause names more than one destination?** Adjacent links, then whitespace-separated links, then a malformed clause inheriting the previous valid target, then a bare or backticked second target, then one after the `§N`. Every round produced a real fix and the next round found the next shape. That is the signature of a missing abstraction, and the adversarial and consistency reviewers independently converged on the same recommendation: ONE shared clause parser, called by every producer, instead of a widening pattern they each match against.

**This section does NOT exist because behaviour is wrong.** An earlier draft claimed three live clauses needed a design decision about multi-destination semantics; that was FALSE and review caught it -- all three are ordinary checklist or prose lines that `STAMP_HEADER_RE`, `STAMP_LINE_RE` and `INPUTS_XREF_RE` all reject. The probe had scanned every line in `todo/` rather than the lines the producers read. Re-measured correctly: **zero** producer-visible clauses name more than one destination, which is exactly why §33 could close the class outright instead of deferring it.

So this is a refactor with a named risk, not a repair. Do it when the fifth producer arrives, or when the pattern next needs widening -- and if neither happens, it is legitimately never urgent.

The four producers are `build.py`'s `XREF_CLAUSE_RE` (stamp targets), `INPUTS_XREF_RE` (Inputs bullets), the table branch inside `extract_inputs_xrefs`, and `validate.py`'s `fix_line_numbers` (the repair path).

- [x] One clause parser, returning the destinations it found
      `cache_schema.parse_xref_clause(text, pos, limit=None)` returns an immutable `XrefClause` -- `targets` (a TUPLE, every destination the clause names), the primary target's span, the section marker and its span, the item name and the parenthetical's span, the consumed end offset, and a `malformed` reason. `XREF_ADJACENT_TARGET_RE` is now the private `_XREF_ADJACENT_TARGET_RE`, reachable only through the parser.
      - `targets` is a tuple rather than a target plus a boolean because the design review's second finding was that a bool records that a caller should refuse WITHOUT recording what it refused -- unusable in a diagnostic and untestable for parity.
      - THE SEAM MOVED IN RESPONSE TO THE DESIGN REVIEW'S FIRST FINDING. The draft kept each producer's grammar and called the parser after it matched, which for the stamp and table surfaces means the parser never sees the malformed clause at all: `build.py:325` bound the target and its REQUIRED section marker in one regex, and the table branch anchored the whole cell, so a two-destination clause simply failed to match. A lead-in that owns the target relocates the drift instead of removing it. Lead-ins now end at `XREF:`.
- [x] Every producer calls it, and a test proves they cannot disagree
      All four route through it: `_walk_stamps_xrefs` (via `XREF_LEAD_RE`), the Inputs bullet (`INPUTS_XREF_LEAD_RE`, cut down to just the lead-in), the Inputs table branch, and `validate.py:fix_line_numbers`. Sub-test 37a runs ONE clause body through all four surface embeddings and requires an identical parse; 37b asserts each producer's own POLICY separately, because the policies legitimately differ and a verdict-only "identical treatment" assertion would pass while two callers bound different destinations.
      - POLICY STAYED LOCAL BY DESIGN: the stamp and table surfaces drop silently, the bullet skips, and the repair path counts the clause, names it in a diagnostic and installs a `None` barrier. The parser reports what it found; it does not decide what that costs. No diagnostic was added at the two silent sites -- that would be new output from a behaviour-preserving refactor.
- [/] The FOURTH live grammar, in `.claude/hooks/skill_step_block.py:61`, adopts the shared parser -- PARKED, control plane, operator-gated
      `.claude/hooks/**` is control plane the unattended run may not edit, so only an attended session can land it; this is a park with a named owner, not an omission.
      - The adopter also needs an import path decision the three producers do not: `cache_schema` lives in `scripts/todo-graph/`, and the hook runs from an arbitrary working directory, so it needs a path bootstrap or a vendored copy -- and a vendored copy would recreate the very duplication this section removed. Raised by this section's design review, which asked for the hook to be tested as a fifth consumer "including import behavior from outside the repository working directory".
      - Reciprocal owner filed at [`overnight-runner-improvements-v14`](../overnight-runner-improvements/overnight-runner-improvements-v14.md) under "Filed by the 2026-08-11 run, TODO-06 section 37 ship".
      - `_XREF_RE = re.compile(r"->\s*XREF:\s*(?:\[[^\]]*\]\()?([\w./0-9-]+\.md)")`. Its label class `[^\]]*` truncates where the shared grammar does not, so the two disagree on a link whose label contains a bracket.
      - Lower stakes than the producers and deliberately parked here rather than patched in place: it feeds a read-XREF heuristic, so a miss under-counts evidence rather than binding a wrong file. Fixing a fourth copy while this consolidation is open is the duplication CLAUDE.md forbids.
      - XREF: transferred from `todo/overnight-runner-improvements/overnight-runner-improvements-v13.md` (item: "A fourth XREF link grammar lives in `skill_step_block.py`, outside the shared one"), closed there 2026-08-10 as owned by this section.
      The parity assertion matters more than the parser: a fixture that runs the SAME malformed clause through all four producers and requires identical treatment is what would have caught rounds 4-7 in one round instead of four.
- [x] Prove the corpus effect per clause before and after
      NIL in both directions, per clause rather than by count: all 1010 stamp targets and 945 Inputs rows byte-identical on the (file, target, section, item) tuple, and the repair path unchanged at 0 updates / 6 ambiguous / 51 unresolvable / 155 missing.
      - TWO CONTROLS, and both fire: forcing the parser to report every clause malformed takes the producers to 0/0, and forcing it to find no clause also takes them to 0/0. The first is the one that matters -- it proves each producer genuinely CONSULTS the shared verdict rather than merely importing the module.
      - ONE ROW MOVED AND WAS PUT BACK. The shared token grammar keeps backticks (194 stamp targets carry them; `resolve_xref_target` strips them at the point of use), while the retired bullet arm excluded them -- so `` `path.md`: description `` gained a trailing `` `: ``. The fix is `build.py:_inputs_target`, ONE code-span rule now shared by both Inputs surfaces, which the two forms previously spelled differently (`` `?([^`\s]+) `` vs a two-ended `strip`).
      - The first probe of this section over-counted, exactly as this section's own preamble warns: scanning every `-> XREF:` line found 3 "multi-destination" clauses, all of them ordinary checklist items no producer reads. Re-measured through the producers themselves: 0.
- [x] Bound the clause at TOP LEVEL, and iterate clauses non-overlapping (post-ship adversarial round 1, [high])
      The first cut searched for the next `-> XREF:` from the clause START, which is context-blind: `-> XREF: [see -> XREF: details](foo.md) §1` cut the clause inside the link label. The boundary is computed after each self-delimiting part (target, item parenthetical) is consumed instead.
      - THE REVIEWER NAMED HALF OF IT. The callers still iterated leads with a plain `finditer`, so an inner lead did not merely cut the clause -- it STARTED one, publishing a fabricated second edge. The retired `XREF_CLAUSE_RE.finditer` never had that exposure because it consumed the whole clause. `cache_schema.iter_xref_clauses` restores non-overlap in one place; 37f pins ASCII and U+2192 spellings in both a label and an item name.
      - Also from this round: no Inputs token may normalise to an empty `target_path` (schema `minLength` 1, and a doubled delimiter cut to nothing -- 37g), and every match is `endpos`-bounded rather than slicing `text[:limit]`, which was quadratic in clause count (37h asserts the ratio, not a wall-clock budget).
- [x] Commit: `"todo-graph: one parser decides how many destinations a clause names"`

**Test checkpoint:** the same malformed clause is treated identically by all four producers; the shapes §33 closed stay closed (adjacent, spaced, bare-plus-link, post-section-marker, and the barrier case); controls -- a `--` description mentioning another TODO, an `(item: ...)` parenthetical, a legitimate multi-XREF stamp line -- are unaffected; the corpus differential is nil; `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` and `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` green.

> **Test runner:** `bash scripts/todo-graph/tests/test_build.sh` -- expect `585/585 passed, 0 failed`, of which 8 are this section's (`37a` four-surface parse identity; `37b` per-producer refusal policy; `37c` the controls -- multi-XREF stamps, prose mentions, item tails, code spans; `37d` the clause bound under a widened adjacency grammar; `37e` a second target after the item parenthetical; `37f` a lead written inside a label or item name; `37g` no shape normalises to an empty target; `37h` linear multi-clause scaling). Sub-tests `33f` and `33g` were repaired to probe the producers instead of their retired regexes. Aggregate via `bash scripts/test-tooling.sh` at `1320/1320`. No kernel test surface: host tooling, so no `TEST_CAT_*` category or bat.

> - The seam is the LEAD-IN boundary, not the constant: each producer hands over the offset past `XREF:` and everything rightwards is the parser's, with policy staying local (silent drop, skip, or count-name-and-barrier).

## 38. Every Gate Parser Adopts the Shared Fence Tracker

> **Spawned-by:** §36 (split)

Split out of §36 at authoring time, before code, once its design review widened the parser inventory from the two originally filed to eleven across six files. The split is by RISK CLASS, not by size: §36 changes what the CACHE says, and this section changes what tools DECIDE -- four parsers here return blocking verdicts that can refuse a commit or a fixpoint. A wrong mask here is a wedged commit, not a wrong count, so each adopter needs its own corpus differential before landing. §36 lands first because it publishes `fence_mask()`; this section is adoption only.

Split AGAIN 2026-08-11, before code, on the sequencer's SPLIT-RECOMMENDED verdict (8 subsystems, waiver declined): the two MUTATING repair tools moved to §41. The axis is the one this section's own text already named, and it is a clean file boundary -- no file is touched by both sections -- because the two halves have DIFFERENT acceptance oracles. A gate adopter is proven by identical blocking VERDICTS across the corpus; a mutating adopter is proven by a byte-identical corpus under `--diff`. The waiver was declined rather than written because §36 -- the same change class against ONE producer -- needed eight review rounds, so "six adopters fit one worker context" was not a prediction that could be made honestly.

- [x] Route the four GATE parsers that return blocking verdicts
      Each of these can refuse a commit or a fixpoint over a literal example in a fence, which is the failure mode that costs a run the most. All four now take the mask from [`scripts/todo_fence.py`](../../scripts/todo_fence.py), a shim over `cache_schema.fence_scan` -- one import shape instead of four `importlib` dances, and one place that reuses an already-imported `cache_schema` rather than loading a second module object for the same source.
      - `scripts/todo-reachability.py:126` (`_sections`) and `:148` (`_io_rows`) -- a fenced `## 99.` splits a real section so its later items are attributed to a section with no IO row, producing a blocking `no-io-row` verdict; this parser gates `phase FIXPOINT`.
      - `scripts/todo-orphan-check.py:66` (`OPEN_ITEM_RE`) and `:69` (`IO_ROW_RE`) -- backs `lint.sh` Check 23, which ERRORs at commit.
      - `scripts/todo-staged-check.py:41` (`_ITEM`) and `:207` (`_SECTION_RE`) -- refuses a staged commit on section shape.
      - `scripts/lint.sh:825` (`IO_ROW_RE.finditer`, pattern at `:735`) and `section_body` (`:773`, whose inline heading regex and `BODY_END_RE` at `:752` bound the slice) -- Checks 10/11 scan raw Markdown under `re.MULTILINE` with no fence state, so a fenced IO row plus a fenced heading can be read as a shipped section and raise a blocking stamp-region or OS-comparison error. Found by §36's round-4 consistency review; it was the gate parser the original inventory missed.
      - Check 11's OS Comparison parsers are a THIRD lint walk, separate from the two above: `os_table_block` (`lint.sh:786`, called at `:824`) bounds the table and `os_row_for_section` (`:796`, called at `:858`) scans its raw rows. A fenced `## OS Comparison` displaces the real table, and a fenced row can satisfy or fail the check on text that is an example.
      - Do NOT route `SEC_HDR_RE` (`lint.sh:741`): it is defined and never consumed, so changing it would be a no-op dressed as a fix. The live heading match is the inline regex inside `section_body`. Caught by §36's round-5 consistency review, which is exactly the shape a line-number-only reference produces. Left untouched, with the reason recorded beside it.
- [x] Make every lint Check 10/11 pattern LINE-LOCAL, because masking a line can otherwise JOIN two real ones
      Found by this section's design review before any code landed, and reproduced with a control: `\s` and `[^|]` both match a newline, so once the lines between two real ones are blanked, a bare `##` line plus a later `12. Title` line satisfies `^##\s+12\.\s+(.+?)$` though nothing matches before masking -- a section the file does not have. `| a | b` plus a real row does the same to the `[^|]*`-based Implementation Order pattern.
      - Every pattern in the block now uses `[ \t]` and `[^|\n]`, so masking can only ever REMOVE a match. The hazard PRE-DATES masking: these patterns could already span lines in an unmasked file, so this is a repair rather than a mitigation for the mask.
      - The fixture asserts the shipped `lint.sh` still uses the line-local forms, over CODE lines only -- the block's own comments name the hazard, and matching those would make the assertion pass or fail on prose.
- [x] Refuse an unclosed fence or `<!--` instead of going quiet on it
      `fence_mask` masks an unclosed opener through EOF, which is fail-CLOSED for a producer and fail-OPEN for a gate: every later item, heading, row and stamp disappears, so the gate reports clean precisely because it could not read the file. `build.py:1030` already REFUSES these documents, so a silent gate would disagree with the producer about the same file. All four now name the delimiter instead.
      - `unclosed_reason()` gives one wording, and reports fence and comment separately, because naming the wrong delimiter sends an author to the wrong line.
      - Measured 2026-08-11 across all 281 files under `todo/`: ZERO carry either construct unclosed, so the refusal costs nothing today. That is the argument for adding it now rather than the first time it fires.
- [x] Read the STAGED post-image in `todo-staged-check.py`, not the working tree
      Also from the design review. `_added_lines` derives every line number from `git diff --cached`, while five structural walks read `pathlib.Path(path)` -- the same file only when nothing is partially staged. A fence mask taken from the wrong snapshot then decides which of those coordinates are fenced, so the gate could ignore a staged section or refuse content the commit does not contain.
      - One `_post_image_lines()` via `git show :<path>` now feeds every walk, falling back to the working tree when git cannot produce the blob (outside a repo, or a path staged as a deletion) -- every caller already fails open on an unreadable file, and a gate that refused outside a work tree would be worse than the drift.
      - Pinned by a fixture that stages one version and leaves a DIFFERENT one in the worktree: an unstaged over-cap item must not refuse, and the same item staged must.
- [x] Give lint Checks 10/11 the same staged snapshot, and let the commit gate scope them
      Found by this section's adversarial review AFTER the staged-check fix landed, which is the same defect one tool over. Checks 10/11 read the worktree while `.githooks/pre-commit:62` runs them under `LINT_GATE_SCOPE_STAGED=1`. The worktree read PRE-DATES this section, but fence-awareness gave it two routes it never had: an unstaged fence around a staged violation MASKS it, and an unstaged unclosed fence REFUSES a commit that does not contain it.
      - Checks 10/11 now honour `gate_excluded()` like Checks 7/17/24, so a run's mid-edit TODO cannot wedge an unrelated commit -- the exact wedge that scoping exists to stop, which the new refusal would otherwise have reintroduced.
      - THE FIRST FIX WAS UNSOUND AND ROUND 2 KILLED IT. Reading the index only for files `git diff` calls modified was rejected on four counts, each an unstaged edit steering a gate that judges a commit: a staged-NEW file deleted from disk is never visited by a filesystem walk; `--name-only` C-QUOTES a newline or non-ASCII path so it can never match the path string it is compared against; `assume-unchanged` / `skip-worktree` / a clean-smudge filter hides a difference from `git diff` entirely; and anything saved between the diff and the read is a plain TOCTOU window.
      - The shipped version enumerates the INDEX instead -- `git ls-files -z -s`, blobs fetched by SHA through one `cat-file --batch`, so a path is never a protocol delimiter and 281 blobs cost one subprocess. An unmerged path or an unreadable blob is an infrastructure ERROR, never a clean bill.
      - Both routes are pinned by fixture: a staged file deleted from the worktree is still judged, and a non-ASCII path whose worktree copy fences the violation is still caught.
- [x] Make lint Check 24 EXHAUSTIVE, and fail closed on a kind it does not know
      Found in this section's own self-review and widened by round 2. Three independent passes keyed on two kind names go silent every time the tool learns a third -- and it had already gone silent twice. The new `unclosed-*` refusal records parsed fine, passed the rc-vs-shape pair, and were counted by nothing; so was `no-io-row`, which is `todo-reachability.py`'s OLDEST kind and the one gating `phase FIXPOINT` -- `grep -n 'no-io-row' scripts/lint.sh` returned nothing at all.
      - One aggregation now emits every bucket, errors on `no-io-row` and on an unreadable file, and ERRORS on any kind not in its known set. Counting handled records against the total is what makes the next new kind loud instead of invisible.
- [x] Read the SECTION column in `_io_rows`, and only where the header declares one
      The rule takes the first all-digit cell of the first two columns, which in the six-column layout is the ORDER, while the caller compares the result against SECTION numbers from the headings. They agree only while a file's two ranges coincide, and this file now runs order 39 -> §41. Measured first: 0 of 281 corpus files currently have a section the old rule missed, so this is a latent defect closed while the function was open.
      - TWO WRONG REPAIRS PRECEDED THE RIGHT ONE, both caught by measurement rather than reasoning, and both worth recording because the reasoning was plausible each time. (1) Collecting the marker IN ADDITION to the order number, on the argument that a bigger set can only remove a false refusal -- false here, because membership IS the integrity check, so an order number satisfies it for an unrelated section. (2) Scanning the whole row for a marker -- the `Depends On` cell carries markers too, which read a row's DEPENDENCY as its own section and produced 59 phantom `no-io-row` refusals corpus-wide.
      - Shipped: the marker is read from the column the HEADER declares `Section`, found by content and never by position. Position was the third wrong answer -- skipping "the first row after the heading" ate the only data row of a header-less table, which disarmed the cache-coverage refusal that `test_build.sh`'s routed-reader fixture exists to prove. Tables with no Section column keep the order-number fallback unchanged.
      - Corpus differential nil at 331 records, and `test_build.sh` back to 586/586.
- [/] `.claude/hooks/sequencer_triage.py:139` (`section_stamps`) and `:169` (`file_lifecycle`) attribute FENCED example stamps to real sections -- PARKED, control plane, operator-gated
      The highest-impact instance of this class, and the one that changes what the runner DOES: `SECTION_HEADING_RE` / `VERIFIED_RE` / `QUALITY_RE` / `DEFERRED_RE` (`sequencer_triage.py:55-63`) all match raw lines with no fence state, across THREE walks -- `section_stamps` (`:139`), `file_lifecycle` (`:169`) and `collect_blockers` (`:262`, whose own inline re-scan at `:274-284` pairs `SECTION_HEADING_RE` with `DEFERRED_RE`).
      - REPRODUCED 2026-08-10: a TODO whose section 1 carries a genuinely open `- [ ]` plus a fenced ```markdown example showing what Verified/Quality stamps look like yields `section_stamps -> {1: {V, Q}}`, so the triage oracle classifies that section DONE and the run SKIPS the open work. `file_lifecycle` has the same exposure for `Validated:` / `Gap-audited:` in the preamble.
      - `.claude/hooks/**` is control plane, so only an attended session can land it. The fix is adoption, not design: route the three walks through `cache_schema.fence_mask`, exactly as `build.py` now does.
      - Reciprocal owner filed at [`overnight-runner-improvements-v13`](../overnight-runner-improvements/overnight-runner-improvements-v13.md) under "Filed by the 2026-08-10 run, TODO-06 section 36 ship".
- [/] `scripts/overnight/section_slice.py:82` (`SECTION_END_RE`, defined `:64`) terminates a real section at a fenced `## N.` example -- PARKED, control plane, operator-gated
      `scripts/overnight/**` is control plane: the unattended run may not edit it, so only an attended session can land this one. Verified by fixture during §32's review. It is a READ path, so the consequence is a truncated section slice rather than a corpus edit or a refused commit.
      - Reciprocal owner filed at [`overnight-runner-improvements-v13`](../overnight-runner-improvements/overnight-runner-improvements-v13.md) under "Filed by the 2026-08-10 run, TODO-06 section 36 ship", so an attended session has a concrete item to act on rather than a park with no re-open path.
- [/] `.claude/hooks/todo_wrap_reminder.py:109` (`_blocks`) and `:177` (`long_continuation`) each carry their own `incode` toggle -- PARKED, control plane, operator-gated
      A FIFTH TODO parser with the naive state machine §41 deleted from `todo-reflow.py`: it toggles on any stripped line starting with three backticks, so it mishandles tilde fences, longer outer runs, closer constraints and comments. Found by §41's post-commit consistency review, which is why it is filed here rather than there -- this section owns the parser adoptions, and §41 owns the two mutating tools.
      - Consequence is an authoring WARNING, not a verdict or an edit: it can nag about a literal fenced example, or miss a real hard-wrap after one. That is why it is a park and not a blocker.
      - `.claude/hooks/**` is control plane the unattended run may not edit. Reciprocal owner filed at [`overnight-runner-improvements-v14`](../overnight-runner-improvements/overnight-runner-improvements-v14.md) under "Filed by the 2026-08-11 run, TODO-06 section 41 ship", so an attended session has a concrete item rather than a park with no re-open path.

All five were `wrapped_block` calling C prototypes and function bodies inside ```c fences "prose hard-wrapped at ~87-132 columns" -- confirmed at file:line in each of the five files. That is the defect being fixed, not a regression, and it is the honest reading of "any changed verdict is the signal".
